In Memoriam 
 
 
 
John Francis Lattanzi was born to John and Frances (Lynch) Lattanzi on April 1, 1936, in Yonkers, 
New York. He grew up in Yonkers and Washington, New Jersey. In his youth John developed a life-
long affinity for mischief, playing rousing piano by ear, and a love of outdoor pursuits. He graduated 
from Lafayette College in 1958, served as an officer in the U.S. Army, then embarked on a 30-year 
career with the American Can Company. 
 
He met his life-long partner, Christine Uhl, in 1961. They married, purchased a camp on Sebec Lake in 
Maine and started their family soon after. John and Christine raised their family in Cranford, NJ and 
Geneva, NY before moving to Lunenburg, MA in 1982. 
 
Over the past 43 years, John served his community in various roles on the Lunenburg Personnel Board, 
Conservation Commission, and Historical Society. John was a member of Saint Boniface and Our Lady 
of the Lake Churches, the Lunenburg Sportsman Club and the Sebec Lake Association. 
 
John leaves behind his beloved wife of over 61 years, and their five children; John Lattanzi Jr. and his 
wife Karen of Honolulu, HI; Jean Proctor and husband Dan of Lunenburg, MA; Peter Lattanzi and wife 
Dina of Cumberland, ME; Paul Lattanzi and wife AnnMarie or Yarmouth, ME; and James Lattanzi and 
wife Allison of Fitchburg, MA. John also leaves behind 15 grandchildren and his younger sister, Jane 
O’Connell, of Durham, NH.

In Memoriam 
 
 
 
 
 Mario A. Patacchiola, 101, of Lunenburg formerly of Somerville, passed away on Wednesday, October 8, 2025 
in the Life Care Center of Leominster. 
 
Mario was born in Cambridge, on December 15, 1923, a son of the late Pasquale and Zelinda (Micille) 
Patacchiola. He graduated from Rindge High School in Cambridge and received his bachelor degree from 
Boston University. 
 
He worked for Bianchi Wedding Dress Company in Boston 
 
Mario was a WWII Veteran having served in U.S. Navy 
 
In 1955 Mario and his wife built a home on Hickory Hills Lake in Lunenburg and would summer there before 
making the move from Somerville to Lunenburg in 1985, and Dunnellon Fl. in the winter. His favorite activity 
was fishing and gardening in Lunenburg and Dunnellon . 
 
Mario was a faithful member of St. Boniface Parish and the Eagle House Senior Center, and a member of the 
American Legion and the AMVETS and Knights of Columbus 
 
His Wife of 79 years, Ann A. (Macone) Patacchiola passed away in 2023. He leaves two sons, Marc Patacchiola 
and his wife Gayle of Burlington and James Patacchiola and his wife Kathleen of Sterling; eight grandchildren, 
eleven great-grandchildren and several nephews and nieces. 
 
He is predeceased by his two sons, David Patacchiola in 2015 and Paul Patacchiola in 1990 + He had 5 brothers 
& sisters, Marco Marino Patacchiola, Helen Petrillo, Edward Patacchiola, Evelyn Rinaldo Walter Patacchiola

===PAGE 7===
 
In Memoriam 
 
 
 
Robert Joseph Saiia of Lunenburg, MA, affectionately known as Bob or Bobby, passed away peacefully on 
April 3, 2025, after a long battle with dementia. Bob was a loving husband to Donna and a devoted father to his 
daughter Tricia Back and son Michael Saiia. 
 
Born in Buffalo, New York (1944), Bob was proud of the days he spent working at The Bankers Life Insurance 
Company in Buffalo, NY~ later Principal Financial Group, where he was known for his dedication and hard 
work. As his career progressed, he was promoted and moved his family to Westerville, Ohio, where he became 
a proud Buckeye fan. His colleagues remember him as a kind and supportive mentor who always had a joke to 
share. He always held dear the memories of his days as a camp counselor at Camp Weona, just outside of 
Buffalo, NY. He also had a passion for coaching, whether it be his own children or once they were grown, 
volunteering as a football coach at Lunenburg High School. Bob also was very proud of the time he served on 
the Lunenburg Planning Board. 
 
Known as the life of the party, Bob valued every friendship, especially his lifelong friends from Frontier High 
School, where he graduated in 1962, affectionately calling themselves, HAAC-Hamburg Attitude Adjustment 
Club. Each and every friendship he made throughout his 81 years of life left an impact on him and he couldn’t 
go anywhere without making a new friend. His infectious laughter and warm spirit made him a beloved friend 
to all. 
 
Bob loved listening to Jazz, Frank Sinatra and Andrea Bocelli. He loved traveling to Italy and Europe with his 
wife Donna, but his favorite place to travel was Indiana where he could spend time with his children and 
grandchildren. He was the best Grandpa around to his two grandchildren, Renee and Anthony; they were his 
pride and joy and he would do just about anything to put a smile on their faces. He was always their biggest 
cheerleader and supporter and loved attending all their various events and activities. Bob adored his time with 
his family and his dogs, loved sports and he especially loved celebrating his Italian heritage. He took great pride 
in his roots and enjoyed sharing stories of his family's traditions and history. 
Bob is survived by his devoted wife, Donna Saiia, daughter Tricia Back (Robert Back), son Michael Saiia, 
grandchildren, Renee Back (19) and Anthony Back (18), stepchildren Christina Gallagher and Michael 
Gallagher, sister Kathy Krasowski, nieces Tracy Ensminger (Jeff Ensminger) and Julie Reinstein (Craig 
Reinstein), Linda Saiia Ross, and his five great nephews and one great niece.

In Memoriam 
 
 
 
 
Elsa (Tocterman) Watson, 92, of Lunenburg died Wednesday, December 17, 2025 at University of 
Massachusetts – Memorial Hospital, Worcester. 
 
Her husband of 52 years, Herman S. Watson, died in May 2012. 
 
Elsa was born in Heilbronn, Germany, September 25, 1933 one of eight children born to Augen and Elsa 
(Wass) Tocterman and was educated in Germany. She moved to Lunenburg 62 years ago. 
 
For over twenty-five years Elsa worked independently as a home health aide in the area. She was a member of 
the Eastwood Club in Fitchburg and had been active in the activities at the Lunenburg and Townsend Senior 
Centers. 
 
She leaves her son and daughter in law, Rev. Jeffrey H. and Dina Watson of Sterling, MA and two grandsons, 
Jeffrey and Michael and Her sister Karin Tocterman in Germany.

LUNENBURG PROFILE

MILES 
Lunenburg was settled in 1718 and incorporated in 1728. It occupies a land area of approximately 26.4 
square miles. Lunenburg is located in north central Massachusetts approximatel y 43 miles from  Bos-
ton, It is bordered on the west by the Town of Ashby and the City of Fitchburg, on the south by the 
Town of Lancaster and the City of Leominster, on the east by the Town of Shirley and on the north by 
the Town of Townsend. 
Local legislative decisions are made by an open town meeting.  Subjet to the legislative decisions 
made by town meeting, the affairs of the town are generally administered by an elected five member 
Select Board and a Town Manager and local school affairs are administered by an elected seven 
member School Committee.
Lunenburg is a semi-rural community located in northern Worcester County. Lake Whalom and the for-
mer Whalom Amusement Park delineated the Whalom District from the remainder of Lunenburg. This 
district was developed as a housing base for many factory workers working in the mills and factories of 
Leominster and Fitchburg. In many ways the characteristics of the Whalom District closely reflect charac-
teristics of these cities more than of the rest of Lunenburg. The Hickory Hills Lake area was developed as 
a private community comprised largely of vacation cottages. Shirley Reservoir is a public water body also 
developed with mainly vacation cottages that have now been modified to year-round residences. 
Commercial and industrial development is permitted in several areas of town, primarily along  the two 
major arteries (Route 2A and 13) and the town's peripheries, due largely to the close proximity of 
Leominster's and Fitchburg's sewer and water systems. Although many farms and orchards have slowly 
developed into single family house, farming continues on in Lunenburg.  In 2013 the Annual Town 
Meeting voted to establish an Agricultural Commission to represent, preserve and enhance farming in 
the commujnity and to administer the "Right to Farm" bylaw.  
In 2014 Lunenburg Town Meeting established the Architectural Preservation District ("District") bylaw 
to regulate construction, demolition or renovation of existing "Contributing Property" structures as 
defined  by the bylaw with an established bounded district.  The bylaw was introduced at Town Meeting 
along with the Village District Bylaw, which created an overlay zoning district to encourage the 
economic vitalization of downtown Lunenburg, the Architectural Preservation District Bylaw ensures 
that it will not come at the expense of the town's historical character and integrity.

REPORT OF THE TOWN MANAGER 
Fiscal Year 2025 
Fiscal Year 2025 saw significant transition in the Office of the Town Manager.  The Office of the Town 
Manager was led by three individuals over the course of the year: Heather Lemieux, who departed in the 
summer of 2024 after eight years of dedicated service; Carter Terenzini, who served ably as Interim Town 
Manager from August 2024 through early February 2025; and Jennifer Warren-Dyment, who was appointed as 
Town Manager and began on February 4, 2025. Throughout these transitions, department heads, Town staff, the 
Select Board, and the many volunteers who serve on our boards and committees provided the continuity and 
commitment that kept Lunenburg moving forward. 
Below are the highlights of the key topics and accomplishments from FY25 within the Office of the Town 
Manager. 
Leadersh
ip Transition 
Town Mana
ger Heather Lemieux submitted her notice to the Select Board in June of 2024.  Her last meeting 
with the Select Board was the evening of July 23, 2024, during which she offered heartfelt remarks on her 
service to the community.  She expressed gratitude to residents, board members, staff, business owners, and 
state officials, reflecting on her seven and a half years of service. She outlined major accomplishments, 
including long-term staffing plans for public safety, significant capital improvements, enhanced ADA 
accessibility, and infrastructure projects such as Complete Streets initiatives, Summer Street improvements, and 
bridge funding. She emphasized that her proudest achievements were the relationships built during her tenure. 
She clarified that her resignation was not requested by the Board and attributed her decision, in part, to a loss of 
trust. She concluded by thanking her family for their support and reiterated her commitment to having served 
Lunenburg with dedication and integrity. 
Carter Terenzini was engaged as Interim Town Manager beginning in August 2024. He brought extensive 
municipal management experience, including several prior engagements in the Interim role, and assumed the 
position immediately.  As Interim Town Manager, Mr. Terenzini not only kept the day-to-day operations 
running smoothly, but also advanced a number of capital projects, completed the obligation of ARPA funds, 
navigated multiple vacancies, and made notable strides in improving employee engagement. At his final Select 
Board meeting on January 28, 2025, Interim Town Manager Terenzini presented his concluding report and was 
thanked by the Board for filling vacant positions, committing ARPA funds, and fostering greater employee 
participation during his tenure. Board members expressed appreciation for his work navigating challenging 
circumstances. 
With the Interim position filled, the Select Board stood up a Town Manager Screening Committee and hired the 
search firm of Community Paradigm Associates.  Per the Town Charter, The Town Manager shall be appointed 
by the Select Board from names submitted to it by a Town Manager Screening Committee which shall consist of 
1 member of the Select Board, designated by it, 1 member of the School Committee, designated by it, 1 member 
of the planning board, designated by it, 1 member of the Finance Committee designated by it and the Town 
Moderator, who shall serve as the chair and call all meetings of the Town Manager Screening Committee. 
Whenever the position of Town Manager shall be vacant, the Town Manager Screening Committee shall be 
convened and shall solicit candidates, review applications, interview and rate candidates and submit the names 
of qualified candidates to the Select Board who shall appoint a Town Manager only from the names so 
prepared and submitted to it. 
The Screening Committee, Chaired by Town Moderator Tim Murphy, also included Tom Alonzo of the Select 
Board, Peter Beardmore of the School Committee, Chris Menard of the Finance Committee and Matt Brenner 
of the Planning Board.  The Committee worked with Community Paradigm to develop the Position Statement 
for the Town Manager Role, screen applications, interview semi-finalist candidates, and recommend a list of 
finalists to the Select Board for their consideration.  Much of the work of the Screening Committee is conducted 
16
===PAGE 21===
in Executive Session. 
Three finalists were publicly interviewed by the Select Board on January 14, 2025.  They included Angeline 
Lopes Ellison, Jennifer Warren-Dyment, and John Woodsmall.  The next evening, the Board moved (4-1) to 
appoint Ms. Warren-Dyment as Town Manager pending successful negotiation. On January 28, 2025, the Select 
Board approved the contract with Ms. Warren-Dyment as Town Manager.  Ms. Warren-Dyment began her first 
day on February 4, 2025, and began immediately engaging with department heads, the Select Board, and the 
budget process that was already well underway. 
Select Board Composition and Goals 
Throughout th
e majority of FY25, the Select Board was composed of Chair Caesar Nuzzolo, Vice-Chair 
Michael-Ray Jeffreys, Clerk Amanda Moore, Tom Alonzo, and Renee Emkey. The Board met regularly, 
typically the first three Tuesdays of the month, and addressed a wide range of Town business including the 
annual and special town meetings, policy updates, the Town Manager transition, budget discussions, licensing 
matters, and appointments. 
In the fall of 2024, the Select Board engaged in goal-setting discussions with Interim Town Manager Terenzini. 
The Board established the following goals (each goal including a number of additional objectives):  
1) Establish policies in collaboration with the Town Manager and Assistant Town Manager/HR Director to
improve the quality of the Town as an employer, addressing the issues of recruitment, retention, and
overall work environment
2) Revamp the Select Board and Town Manager Goal-Setting Process
3) Increase Transparency and Availability of Information to the Public
4) Work through the Town Manager, Assistant Town Manager/HR Director and the department heads to
initially create, to periodically review and to develop and secure plans, policies and procedures
Financial Matters 
FY26 Budget Development 
Interim Town Manager Terenzini began the FY26 budget process, working with the departments to develop 
budget requests and with Finance Director/Town Accountant Ezequiel Ayala to develop revenue projections.  
Town Manager Warren-Dyment continued the FY26 budget process after her arrival. She held meetings with all 
departments in January and worked to understand the picture of revenues and expenditures and made initial 
revenue adjustments.   
By March 2025, the FY26 budget picture had come more fully into sharper focus. The FY26 budget presented 
to the Select Board on March 19, 2025, included reductions across a number of areas, including cuts to the 
Pavement Management Plan (which were offset by Chapter 90 funding). 
On May 3, 2025, a $49,374,465,00 balanced budget was presented and approved at Town Meeting.  The budget 
was subsidized by a $500,000 appropriation of free cash to the Lunenburg Public Schools (LPS) to fund FY25 
and avoid an override allowing town and school leaders to have time to jointly plan for FY27. 
The need for additional state funding was a theme that persisted through the development of the FY26 budget 
with town and school leaders advocating for increased Chapter 70 and Unrestricted General Government Aid 
funding.   
Budget Task Force 
The Budget Task Force, formed by the Select Board, brought forward recommendations that were reviewed at 
17
===PAGE 22===
the February 11, 2025 meeting.  The Task Force concluded there were three structural causes to the state of 
disrepair in Lunenburg: 1) Proposition 2 ½ funding; 2) lack of competitive employment packages; and 3) an 
overly complex financial structure for the town.   
Recommendations of the Task Force included 1) consolidating town investment accounts and funds; 2) a 
targeted diagnostic audit; 3) implement the prudent investment rule; 4) review the viability of the Community 
Preservation Act; 5) Green grants for HVACs; 6) solar on old town dump; 7) solar on school lots; 8) Brooks 
House lease; 9) review trash and recycling programs; 10) designate additional roads as town roads; 11) 
implement a high deductible health plan; 12) increase health insurance opt outs; 13) payroll and purchase order 
streamlining; 14) standardize and improve workflow for town finances; 15) review school solar contracts; 16) 
create regular review process for school fees; 17) create regular review process for town fees; 18) change 
warrant delivery; 19) consolidate town and school snow removal and ground maintenance; and 20) determine 
viability of split tax rate. 
Other Financial Mentions 
Town Manager Lemieux had obtained a grant to work with the Division of Local Services (DLS) to draft an 
update Lunenburg’s financial policies and procedures which were not adopted prior to her departure.  Interim 
Town Manager Terenzini shared the draft and encouraged feedback and commentary.  This project was not 
completed in FY25.   
As of March 2025, the Town had received over $225,000 in cumulative opioid settlement funds. Town Manager 
Warren-Dyment began preliminary discussions with the Police Chief and Fire Chief on the potential use of 
these funds.  No funds were expended in FY25.   
A final tax rate of $14.36 per thousand was established for FY25.  Free cash was certified at $2,270,060.   
Infrastructure, Facilities, and Capital Projects 
Flat Hill Road Bridge 
The Flat Hill Road Bridge replacement over Catacoonamug Brook was the most significant and most 
challenging capital project of FY2025. After years of planning, the project, funded by a previously secured 
$500,000 Small Bridge Grant and supplemental MassDOT funding, was awarded to Kenefick Corporation. The 
road was closed beginning March 7, 2025, with an anticipated reopening of April 25, 2025 and a detour route 
put in place and communicated widely to the public.  However, the water level in the lake had already risen 
significantly by the time work was to begin, and Kenefick’s water control plan proved inadequate for the 
conditions. No work was performed on the structure, and Kenefick submitted a request for a one-year extension, 
which was denied.  The bridge remained closed for the remainder of FY25 after a MassDOT Underwater Bridge 
Inspection Team inspection found additional section in the structure.   
Town Building Projects 
• The Town Hall Roof Replacement and Chimney Restoration project was one of the major capital
investments of FY25. The contract was issued in December 2024 under Interim Town Manager
Terenzini, with the chimneys prioritized first as they were anticipated to generate the most change
orders. Winter weather slowed progress, but by March 2025 both Town Hall chimneys were complete
and the roofing work had begun. By April 2025 the Town Hall roof was nearly complete. The contractor
then transitioned to the Ritter Memorial Building, completing chimney repointing, flashing repairs, slate
tile replacement, and gutter and trim work. The project was substantially complete by May 2025.
• The Library Teen Room expansion was completed during FY25. The project included construction of
two new offices (Phase 1), expansion of the teen room itself (Phase 2), and replacement of the children’s
story craft room floor.
18
===PAGE 23===
• The Adult Activity Center kitchen renovation, a long-delayed FY21 capital project, was completed 
during FY25. The renovation included a new stove, warming oven, stainless sink, stainless counters, and 
a grease trap installation. A rotted sub-slab drain pipe was discovered during construction and repaired, 
delaying the kitchen’s reopening, but the kitchen was back in full use by May 2025. 
• A $185,000 Townwide Keying and Security project was included in the FY25 Capital Plan to replace all 
exterior locks and upgrade access control systems at Town Hall, the Ritter Memorial Building, the 
Public Safety Building, the Adult Activity Center, the Library, the DPW facility, the Beach House, and 
the Teen Center. A contract with Dugmore and Duncan was executed in the Spring of 2025, but the 
project was not completed within the Fiscal Year.   
• The DPW Floor Project and DPW Roof Project both commenced in December 2024.  
• The Senior Center Roof project was completed in the fall of 2024. 
ARPA Fund Obligations 
Completing the obligation of all American Rescue Plan Act (ARPA) funds before the federal deadline was one 
of Interim Town Manager Terenzini’s most urgent and consequential priorities during FY2025. The Town had 
received approximately $3.5 million in ARPA Coronavirus State and Local Fiscal Recovery Funds (SLFRF), 
and the federal requirement had evolved from a definition of “committed” funds to requiring that all dollars be 
under contract by December 31, 2024. This created a compressed and challenging timeline.  
The ARPA-funded project portfolio being moved to contract during FY25 included a wide range of municipal 
investments including supplementing existing projects on prior year capital plans as well as additional facilities 
and infrastructure side projects, public safety upgrades, school projects, Water District projects, software 
upgrades, and funding for community programs.   
By the end of December, all funds were obligated.  When Interim Town Manager Terenzini presented his final 
report to the Select Board on January 28, 2025, he cited the successful commitment of ARPA funds as one of 
the signature accomplishments of his tenure. Chair Nuzzolo drafted a press release to communicate to the 
public the full scope of ARPA investments the Town had made on behalf of the community.   
 
Personnel 
 
Staffing remained an ongoing challenge and priority throughout FY25. Key staffing actions during FY25 
included: the completion of the Police Chief search and appointment; continued recruitment of the Land Use 
Director; the search and recruitment of the Senior Center Director; the filling of the Assistant to the Sewer 
Business Manager position; the recruitment for the Conservation Agent; a vacancy in the position of Executive 
Assistant to the Town Manager and Select Board to name a few.   
One of the most significant recruitments of FY25 was the search for a new Police Chief following Chief 
Thomas Gammel’s retirement. Applications for the Police Chief position closed in early February 2025. A 
Screening Committee comprised of the Fire Chief, COA Director, Superintendent, a Chief from another 
community, a Town Manager from another community, and Town Manager Warren-Dyment reviewed all 
applications and conducted interviews on March 12, 2025. Promoted from Lieutenant and then Interim Chief, 
Jeffrey Thibodeau, was sworn in as Lunenburg’s Police Chief at a well-attended swearing-in and pinning 
ceremony held on April 18, 2025.  Select Board Chair Caesar Nuzzolo served as Master of Ceremonies and 
PACC produced a video of the ceremony for those unable to attend in person. 
After 30 years of service, Sue Doherty retired as COA Director on December 31, 2024.  After a recruitment 
process, Deb Goodsell was hired to take over the helm at the Center.  Deb brought significant experience to the 
position, having served in Clinton for ten years as their Director.   
After a sustained vacancy in the role of Land Use Director that was vacated by Adam Burney, Christopher 
Reilly was hired in September 2024. The position was vacated again in April 2025 and recruitment continued 
19
===PAGE 24===
for the duration of FY25. 
Community 
The Town of
 Lunenburg celebrated several meaningful community milestones during FY 2025. The swearing-
in ceremony for new Police Chief Thibodeau in April 2025 was a widely attended and celebratory event. The 
Agricultural Commission hosted a well-received agricultural fair at the TC Passios grounds in October 2024. 
The Lunenburg Vendor Fair, organized by the Lunenburg Business Association and Parks Department, was 
held for the first time on May 10, 2025, at TC Passios and featured a number of vendors, food trucks, live 
music, a petting zoo, and other activities. 
Through volunteer efforts, a bocce court was installed at the Adult Activity Center and was completed in 
roughly an hour and a half in the Fall of 2024, with Facilities Director Chris Ruth coordinating the effort.  In 
April 2025, the Council on Aging and the Town celebrated Charles Sanderson, World War II veteran and 
remarkable 101-year-old Lunenburg resident, with a presentation of the Boston Post Cane and a formal 
welcome to the Century Club. 
Closing Remarks 
Fisca
l Year 2025 was a year that asked much of the Town of Lunenburg and of everyone who serves it. 
Through a leadership transition that spanned three managers and the loss of transfer of knowledge that goes 
with such a transition, an active list of capital projects in various stages, continued budgetary pressures, and the 
ongoing work of keeping a community running, the Town’s staff, elected and appointed officials, and 
volunteers continued to show up with dedication and care. 
Thank you to the members of the Select Board that served in FY25: Caesar Nuzzolo, Michael-Ray Jeffreys, 
Amanda Moore, Tom Alonzo, Renee Emkey, Brian Lehtinen, and Glenn McLeod for the leadership, 
engagement, and collaboration.   
Thank you to all department heads who have served for the continuity and leadership which provided stability 
throughout the year.  Thank you to the staff that work in all of our departments for the work that you do.  You 
are our unsung heroes that keep the operations of the town going.   
And thank you to the many volunteers who serve on Lunenburg’s boards, committees, and commissions.  A few 
special thank you to those who assisted in the transition of not only town leadership, but also transitions within 
their departmental areas: Matt Brenner, Planning Board Chair; Chris Menard, Finance Committee Char; and 
Karin Menard, Parks Commission Chair.   
Most importantly- to the residents and businesses who make Lunenburg a community worth serving every day- 
thank you. 
Respectfully submitted, 
Jennifer Warren-Dyment 
Town Manager,  
Town of Lunenburg

PROOF UNDESIGNATED FUND BALANCE  
AGREES TO THE BALANCE SHEET  
BOARD OF
 ASSESSORS 
Under the umbrella of Massachusetts state and local town laws, Lunenburg's Assessing Organization will work 
with town operations and our citizens to meet our vision for delivering a fair and equitable taxation.  The 
Lunenburg Board of Assessors will evolve and administer a series of “Best Practices” methodologies consistent 
with our Vision. These practices will ensure equitable treatment and compassion for all of our citizens. 
The Boar
d of Assessors has empowered an Assessor Organization that has the capability to evaluate and assess 
all property at its full and fair market value.  This is accomplished through a rigorous measurement of property, 
collection of appropriate data, analysis and maintenance of all property tax data records. Additionally, the 
Assessor Department administers a motor vehicle excise tax, real estate and motor vehicle tax abatements, and 
all statutory tax exemptions as allowed by law. The Assessor Organization will work cohesively with other 
town departments. 
The las
t year has seen a lot of progress in restoring the fully functional Assessors’ Office.  
• We conti
nue to be fully staffed after several years of being understaffed. Our staff includes a full time
Principal Assessor, a 19-hour Data Collector and a 32-hour Assessing Administrative Assistant.
• The Assessing office and files have been reorganized from the disarray in 2023.
• Assessing Policies and Procedures have been established.
• A sales inspection program has been established to follow-up on sales and review property data in MLS.
Permit inspections are conducted weekly and a cyclical program has been established with the
inspection of over 500 properties annually to comply with the DOR Directives from the 2024
revaluation.
• We successfully improved several exemptions by loosening eligibility income and asset limits
qualification for Seniors and increasing exemptions amounts.  We also worked on developing a new
“Senior Means Tested Tax Exemption” for implementation in the fall of 2025 (FY2026).
The Boar
d of Assessors’ efforts at restoring the trust of Lunenburg taxpayers include scheduling all our 
meetings during evenings (with both PACC coverage and Zoom access) so everyone can participate more 
easily.  The Board has also developed a Property Tax program for use at the Senior Center and has held a yearly 
senior tax exemption presentation to create awareness of the available real estate tax exemptions for the seniors.   
We continue to improve visibility and access by redesigning of the Website, including analytics and 
explanations provided the Massachusetts DOR / Division of Local Services, Massachusetts Association of 
Assessing Officers, and UMass among others.  The office staff strives to provide exceptional service that the 
Lunenburg taxpayers deserve.

BUILDING DEPARTMENT
The Town of Lunenburg Building Department is Located in the Ritter Memorial Building located at 960 
Massachusetts Avenue. The Building Department works to efficiently, effectively, and consistently ensure a 
safe, sustainable, and vibrant community through effective oversight of construction and development activities 
by enforcing the Massachusetts state Building, Plumbing, Gas, and Electrical Codes, The Architectural Access 
Board Regulations, and Lunenburg’s Zoning Bylaw and Town Code. The Building Department also plays a 
critical role in assisting business through the process of obtaining permits and ensuring compliance with all 
relevant codes and regulations. A commitment to being accessible, responsive and to providing a high level of 
customer service empowers us to be a partner the community, property owners, businesses, contractors, and 
developers can rely on when needed. The Building Department maintains this commitment by:
• Ensuring t
he public has consistent access to effective and courteous assistance in obtaining information,
permits or approvals related to construction, building or zoning regulations and providing clear
information about regulations, permitting requirements and associated fees.
• Maintaining a consistent level of communication by responding to all inquiries and applications in a
timely manner.
• Enforcing building codes, regulations, local codes, and safety standards to
guarantee the safety of occupants and the community.
• Improving and optimizing the permitting process to facilitate efficient and timely approvals for
construction projects while maintaining thorough and consistent review procedures.
• Utilizing technology to streamline internal processes, enhance data management, and provide better
online services for applicants and property owners.
• Regularly reviewing and assessing departmental processes and policies, seeking opportunities for
improvement, efficiency, and effectiveness in serving the community.
• Coordinating with other departments to ensure a streamlined approach to permitting and enforcement as
needed.
• Receiving and evaluating feedback from the public and other interested parties, and implementing
changes as needed to ensure the department goals are being met.
•

CEMETERY COMMISION 
 
Throughout the past year, the Cemetery Commission met regularly with DPW Director William Bernard and 
Cemetery Superintendent Todd Holman to review day‑to‑day operations, maintenance needs, and long‑term 
planning for Lunenburg’s public burial grounds. The Commission also conducted several on‑site visits to North, 
South, and Fish Street Cemeteries to assess current conditions and identify upcoming needs. 
 
The Cemetery Commission is an elected three‑member board with staggered three‑year terms. Its 
responsibilities include establishing cemetery regulations, overseeing care and management of public burial 
grounds, and laying out lots and pathways in both existing and newly acquired cemetery land. Daily operations 
are handled by the Town Manager and delegated to the DPW Director, as the Cemetery Division operates 
within the Department of Public Works. 
 
Overview of Cemetery Properties 
• North Cemetery (Holman Street) 
Approximately 22 acres, with about 10 currently active. The property includes open land suitable for 
near‑term expansion and additional wooded areas for long‑term development.  23-acre wetlands parcel 
also borders the site. 
• South Cemetery (Page Street) 
About 6 active acres, with limited remaining expansion capability. 
• Fish Street Cemetery (Fish & Lincoln Streets) – Goodrich Family 
A historic 0.06‑acre family plot with five marked graves and additional identified burial locations. 
• West Street Terrace – Arnold, Litch, & Moulten Families 
A small historic family cemetery located on private property.  
 
Both North and South Cemeteries remain open for lot sales and burials. 
 
Projects and Improvements 
Significant progress was made on the North Cemetery backlot expansion this year. Town Meeting appropriated 
$50,000 in May 2025, and the Commission contracted with Dillis & Roy for engineering services. Preliminary 
plans were submitted.  The bidding process for construction will be in Fiscal Year 2026. 
 
Administrative improvements included purchasing a secure file cabinet for cemetery records and continuing 
support for the CIMS software system, which offers digital mapping, ownership records, and public search 
tools. 
 
Maintenance efforts included: 
• Rebuilding roughly 150 feet of stone wall, completed by Amanda Construction at the South Cemetery. 
• New concrete pad for flag poles at the South Cemetery, completed by Mike McAllister. 
• Installation of 20 new cedar trees to replace aging hedges by Lakeview Landscaping at the North 
40
===PAGE 45===
Cemetery. 
• Roadside trimming by Ray Morin and front hedge work by Beaulac Brothers 
• Roadway crack sealing by Indus, Inc at both North and South Cemeteries. 
All work was performed under the supervision of the Cemetery Superintendent. 
 
Acknowledgments 
The Commission extends its sincere appreciation to DPW Director William Bernard, Cemetery Superintendent 
Todd Holman, and all individuals and contractors who supported cemetery maintenance and improvements this 
year. Special thanks to: 
• Peter Schmidt and James Versoi, Seasonal Laborers 
• Robin Verenza – natural plantings and landscape guidance

• Jim Lattanzi – donated water tank for watering hedges 
 
Online cemetery maps for North and South Cemeteries are available at www.burialsearch.com.

CONSERVATION 
The Town of
 Lunenburg Conservation Commission is a seven-member commission, each appointed to 3-year 
terms by the Select Board, whose duties are codified under the Conservation Commission Act MGL chapter 40 
s 8C. The Commission enforces the Massachusetts Wetland Protection Act MGL chapter 131 s 40 and Chapter 
239 of the local bylaws for the protection of wetlands.  The Commission also manages, and controls town 
owned lands retained for conservation and passive recreation use. 
The fis
cal year 2025-26 was a very busy and active agenda containing both public hearings and meetings for a 
wide range of projects from commercial development, subdivisions and small single family upkeep projects and 
larger scale industrial/commercial proposals.  The Commission also held numerous inspections on weekends 
prior to meetings to familiarize themselves with the upcoming proposals that would appear on the agenda. 
During this period the Conservation Commission supported 28 NOIs, 36 Administrative Determinations, 18 
RDAs, 34 COCs, and numerous other requests.  
The Conse
rvation Commission continues to implement forest stewardship plans developed using grants as 
outlined in the last annual reports. The funding from the sale of harvested timber provides the seed money to 
make it possible for the Commission to apply for grants to maintain and improve Lunenburg’s Conservation 
Lands. The Conservation Commission continues to execute on the awarded MASS Recreational Trail Program 
Grant that was awarded in 2023. The purpose of this grant was to fund the engineering design and permitting 
work to create parking access in Table Rock and Rayno Conservation Areas. The grant scope also covered 
improving Accessible Access to the Cowdrey Conservation Area as accessed from Townsend Harbor Road. In 
early 2025, the permits were issued for Table Rock and Rayno Areas. This effort is ongoing to complete the 
engineering design and permitting work in the Cowdrey. Members of the commission applied for two additional 
grants for the upcoming year. 
42
===PAGE 47===
 
The Pollinator Habitat portion of the Hollis Rd property provides for a diversity of native flowers throughout 
the growing season. The Habitat promotes insect egg laying and nesting sites for pollinators. The Habitat 
provides food sources for birds and numerous insects including native bees and butterflies. This unique “Hidden 
Gem” works as an ambassador for native pollinators to our community. We are very thankful for the dedicated 
volunteers supporting the Lunenburg Community Pollinator Habitat.   
 
The Conservation Commission will sponsor April Earth Day projects across Conservation Properties. The 
Commission would like to express our gratitude for all the hard-working volunteers that work so 
enthusiastically in maintaining and improving Lunenburg’s conservation lands!  
 
The Commission has also continued to work closely with Lake Shirley and Hickory Hills on the implementation 
of their lake management plans.  These plans set goals and action items designed to give a better picture of the 
overall health of the lakes and define various strategies to ensure continued protection of these valuable 
resources. Both lakes presented to the commission in early 2026. 
 
The administrative office position was removed from the Clerical Union at Town Meeting and moved to SAP 
with a new title of Conservation Agent. This helped the office attract and extremely qualified full-time agent 
and the Commission looks forward to full time office support. 
 
The Commission thanks all the town agencies for their continued support! 
 
Respectfully Submitted, 
Jenny Pewtherer, Chair 
Richard Bursch, Vice-Chair 
Michael Larouche, Clerk 
Mary Wilson, Andrea Burnap, and Teddy Doucette 
 
 
COUNCIL ON AGING 
 
The mission of the Council on Aging is to advocate for the improved quality of life for all residents age 50+ 
through supportive services, programs, and education. Councils on Aging were established under Massachusetts 
General Law Chapter 40, section 8B, to include setting policy for the administration of elder programs or 
services and to develop, coordinate, and conduct such activities which advocate for this population’s issues.  
 
The FY25 Council on Aging staff consisted of Susan Doherty/Debra Goodsell, Director; Michele Beauvais, 
Administrative Assistant and Transportation; Pauline Roy, Outreach Coordinator; Pamela Myllykangas, Meal 
Site Coordinator; Sandra Ricci, Asst. Meal Site Coordinator; Elsa Watson and Ann Penney, Meal Site 
Assistants; Jim McGuigan, David Gallagher, & Kimberly Moore, MART Van Drivers.    
 
The Council on Aging Board is an eleven-member board appointed to 3-year terms by the Lunenburg Select 
Board. The FY24 Council on Aging Board members were Chairperson Deb Lincoln, Vice-Chairperson Jane 
Rabbitt, Ana Lockwood, Pete Lincoln/Jack Rabbitt, Jim McGuigan, Diane Nowd, Cathy Beale, Susan 
O’Sullivan, Elena Christopher, Judith Tarbell/Lou Franco & Helen Balcom.   
 
The Town budget alone does not support the Lunenburg COA at the Lunenburg Adult Activity Center. Other 
sources of funding include the Eagle House Supporters Inc., a non-profit 501c3 group, Formula Grant funding 
from the Commonwealth of Massachusetts, and Montachusett Regional Transit Authority.   
 
In FY23 the Eagle House Senior Community Center rebranded its name to the Lunenburg Adult Activity 
43
===PAGE 48===
Center. The change has encouraged all people aged 50+ to visit the center and participate in programming.  
FY25 showed a continued increase in the number of daily participants.  Growth is anticipated through 2050. 
 
Construction was completed on a new commercial kitchen in the Spring of 2025 using funds that had been 
previously allocated to this project.   
 
We are incredibly lucky to have a large volunteer force at the Council on Aging. In FY25, we had forty-two 
(42) volunteers who contributed 668 hours of their time to help us out in various ways, such as driving for the 
Meals on Wheels program, program leaders, kitchen helpers, Memory Café, office help, library, handyman, and 
friendly visitors/phone calls. We greatly appreciate their service.  
In FY25, we offered a variety of programs including Meals on Wheels, congregate lunches and breakfast 
programs, transportation, health and wellness, arts and crafts, bocce, cards, and games, cultural, Veterans 
Breakfast, movies, Historical Society presentations, information and referral, Memory Café, health insurance 
counseling, and social events both at the Adult Activity Center and offsite to individuals age 50+.  
 
There were approximately 1184 people who utilized the Adult Activity Center in FY25. Those 1184 people 
compiled 22,871 units of duplicated service by participating in the programs listed above.    
 
The Lunenburg Council on Aging newsletter lists current events and is available for you online at 
www.mycommunityonline.com , or you can pick up a copy at the Lunenburg Adult Activity Center, Town Hall, 
Library, Post Office, local churches, and various businesses throughout the community.  
 
We are truly fortunate to have a Town Government that supports our center and understands the importance of 
supporting the Council on Aging to maintain quality services for our residents as they age.

new living room furniture, office/kitchen supplies, various programs & entertainment, and garden upkeep at the 
Lunenburg Adult Activity Center. Also special thanks to all our outside agencies, especially the Lunenburg 
Police and Fire Departments, for their support of programming for our clients.

to our clients. Moving forward, the Lunenburg Council on Aging remains committed to bringing new and 
innovative programming for those aged 50+ in our community while maintaining long lasting relationships with 
those who we serve now and in the future.

DEPARTMENT OF PUBLIC WORKS 
 
The Department of Public Works is honored to serve the residents of Lunenburg each day. Our work touches 
nearly every part of community life—from safe roads and reliable sewer services to well-maintained cemeteries 
and a clean environment. The DPW is made up of the Highway, Cemetery, Sewer, and Vehicle Maintenance 
Divisions, all supported by a dedicated team of public works professionals who take great pride in caring for 
our town. 
 
Our staff includes one Director, one Executive Assistant (shared with Facilities), one Highway Superintendent, 
three Heavy Equipment Operators, two Driver/Laborers, one Mechanic, one Cemetery Superintendent, two 
Seasonal Cemetery Laborers, one Sewer Business Manager, and one Assistant to the Sewer Business Manager. 
44
===PAGE 49===
 
Our Mission 
 
We are committed to delivering high-quality, cost-effective, and safe services that preserve Lunenburg’s 
infrastructure and enhance residents’ daily lives. Our work supports a healthy, welcoming community while 
ensuring responsible management of the Town’s roadway, stormwater, solid waste, and wastewater systems—
today and for generations to come. 
 
Goals and Priorities 
 
As a department rooted in community service, our goals focus on reliability, preparedness, and continuous 
improvement: 
• Provide round-the-clock response to weather events, roadway hazards, and emergencies. 
• Support a skilled and dedicated staff committed to professionalism and public service. 
• Deliver proactive maintenance to reduce long-term costs and improve reliability:  
1. Uphold roadway improvements guided by the 2022 Pavement Management Plan. 
2. Repair and maintain sidewalks per the 2020 PROW ADA Transition Plan. 
3. Expand roadside mowing and vegetation care. 
4. Increase catch basin cleaning and roadway sweeping per the 2020 Operations and Maintenance 
plan to meet MS4 Permit requirements. 
5. Maintain and upgrade the sewer system and pump stations. 
• Expand staff training to help reduce insurance premiums. 
• Identify new state and federal funding sources to stretch tax dollars further. 
• Continue open, respectful, and helpful communication with residents, boards, and commissions. 
• Modernize operations with improved equipment, technology, and asset management. 
 
Highway Division 
 
The Highway Division plays a vital role in keeping our community safe and connected. Their daily work 
includes maintaining roads, drainage, signage, trees, roadside areas, and sewer infrastructure, as well as 
assisting other departments when needed. Our team responds to emergencies day and night to ensure the safety 
of residents. 
 
The DPW Director reviewed permits for street openings, trench safety, sewer connections, driveway 
installations, stormwater BMPs, demolition, and more—ensuring projects throughout town meet safety and 
infrastructure standards. 
 
Pavement Management 
 
Preserving our road network remains a top priority. Following the 2022 Pavement Management Plan, the 
following roads were paved with hot mix asphalt in 2025: 
• Holman Street (from closed bridge to Chase Road) 
• Eastern Avenue 
• Mulpus Road 
• Section of Kilburn Street 
• Section of Reservoir Road 
These improvements help ensure safer travel and extend roadway life while protecting taxpayer investment. 
 
Stormwater Management 
 
Stormwater continues to be a growing challenge statewide, and Lunenburg is no exception. Working alongside 
the Storm Water Task Force and our consultant, the DPW continues to strive to meet EPA and MassDEP MS4 
45
===PAGE 50===
permit requirements. 
Our responsibilities include: 
• Catch basin cleaning
• Street sweeping
• Pipe jetting and replacement
• Swale maintenance
• BMP maintenance
• Responding to flooding and drainage calls
This wor
k plays an important role in protecting water quality and reducing storm-related hazards. 
Bridges
 and Culverts 
In 2025, we c
ompleted a Bridge Assessment and Ranking Plan, outlining prioritized repairs for six bridges and 
culverts. This plan helps the Town coordinate future work efficiently and submit stronger applications for state 
and federal funding.

Lunenburg’s
 roadside trees are a cherished part of our landscape, but they also require ongoing attention. The 
DPW continues to manage tree hazards using the 2015 Tree Risk Management Inventory as a guide, while also 
responding to new issues caused by aging trees and severe storms.

ack specialized equipment such as a bucket truck or crane, larger removals are outsourced. We are

===PAGE 51===
 
Lake Shirley Dam 
 
The Lake Shirley Dam continues to be well maintained and fully compliant with state requirements. The dam is 
inspected regularly by a licensed engineer, and we are grateful to the Lake Shirley Association volunteers for 
many years of dedicated support. 
 
Winter Operations 
 
Snow and ice response is one of our most visible and vital responsibilities. Our team worked tirelessly through 
winter storms to keep roads safe and passable. 
 
Key points from this winter: 
• Lunenburg participates in a regional salt purchasing group to control costs, despite annual price 
increases. 
• Due to aging equipment, we relied more heavily on contracted plow vendors to ensure adequate storm 
coverage. 
• Four new snow-fighting trucks were ordered in 2024; two were delivered in 2025, with two more 
expected in 2026. 
 
We deeply appreciate the hard work of our DPW staff and our private contractors—our community’s true 
“Storm Troopers.” 
 
Cemetery Division 
 
The Cemetery Division provides respectful and meticulous care for our town cemeteries. The Superintendent 
oversees operations, assisted by seasonal workers and supported by the Highway Division as needed.  31 
cremations and 7 full burials were performed this year. 
 
We extend sincere thanks to the Massachusetts Department of Corrections crews and to the many local 
businesses that contributed services, including Lakeview Landscaping, Beaulac Brothers, Dillis and Roy Civil 
Design Group, Leominster Monument, Vitelli Monument, Lunenburg Dipping Donuts, and Tomatoes Pizza.  
And special thanks to Jean Russell, John Bowen, Jr, Chase Jasma, Lunenburg Water Department, and Jim 
Lattanzi for their contributions. 
 
Wastewater 
 
The DPW oversees daily operations of the Town’s wastewater collection system. CTE, a specialized vendor, 
maintains our ten pump stations. 
 
We thank Paula Bertram for her dedicated service and wish her well in her new role. We also welcome Andrew 
Valliere, who joined us in 2025 as Sewer Business Manager, handling billing, customer support, permitting, and 
budgeting while working closely with the Sewer Commission. 
 
Fleet Maintenance 
 
Our Mechanic maintains and repairs more than 30 pieces of equipment, as well as police vehicles, fire 
apparatus, mowers, and other Town assets. Specialized repairs are outsourced as needed, ensuring reliability 
across all departments. 
 
Closing Thoughts

I would like to thank former Town Manager Heather Lemieux for her leadership and wish her well in her next 
chapter. I also appreciate the efforts of Interim Town Manager Carter Terenzini and warmly welcome Jennifer 
Warren-Dyment as our new Town Manager. 
 
Finally, I want to express my deep appreciation to the Selectboard, Town leadership, staff, commissioners, and 
especially the residents of Lunenburg. Your ongoing support allows us to carry out our mission with pride. I am 
profoundly grateful to the DPW staff for their dedication, professionalism, and commitment—much of their 
hard work happens behind the scenes, yet it is essential to the quality of life we all enjoy. 
 
Together, we will continue strengthening the town’s infrastructure and ensuring a safe, vibrant community for 
all.

FINANCE COMMITTEE 
On May 3, 2025, members of the public gathered in the Lunenburg Middle/High School and participate in the 
centuries-long tradition of New England style town government. As part of this tradition, the Finance 
Committee has reviewed all monied articles, recommended updates, held public hearings, and has voted on our 
official recommendations. This warrant contains five budgetary articles that are worth highlighting: 
• Article 10: Omnibus Budget article that provides most funds for the operations of town (both municipal 
and schools). 
• Article 12: One-time, free cash funding to the School Department, breaking from Lunenburg’s 
traditional practices but potentially necessary to fund essential school services. 
• Article 11: A Citizens Petition requesting a Proposition 2 ½ override of $2,099,337. 
• Article 28: A Debt Exclusion article requesting $13.8M in bonds for construction and  revitalization 
following Master Plan of Marshall Park. 
• Article 29: A Debt Exclusion Article requesting between $21,409,100 and $22,301,145 in bonds to 
renovate Town Hall into a Meeting House, renovate Ritter Memorial, and add a Town Hall addition onto 
Ritter Memorial. 
The first three articles are related to operational budgets and service levels for the Town. This report will 
summarize the impact on planned operations, and detailed presentations will be given at Town Meeting. The 
last two articles are debt exclusion articles resulting from years of planning and design work by two 
commissions. 
Town Revenue Projections 
Lunenburg’s revenue comes from multiple sources, primarily Property Tax. This year’s budget builds on the 
FY25 Levy Limit of $31,602,108, including increases from the successful FY25 Proposition 2 ½ override. By 
law, the levy limit increases automatically by 2.5%, resulting in an increase of $790,052.70. Additionally, the 
Town estimates new growth of $400,000, leading to a total increase of 3.76%. Previously approved excluded 
debt of $2,213,677 is then applied, resulting in a maximum allowable levy of $35,005,838. 
FY2026 Net State Aid is estimated at $10,006,739, based on the Governor's budget, with potential increases 
pending State Legislator’s negotiations. Chapter 70 Governor’s budget is set at $9,109,185, reflecting ongoing 
challenges with state aid for education. The Chapter 70 program is the primary source of state aid for funding 
48
===PAGE 53===
local public elementary and secondary schools. From FY13-FY23 state aid increased at an annual rate of 5.3%, 
but in the last three years, it has slowed to 1.5%, increasing the financial burden on the town to raise and 
appropriate funds. From a Lunenburg resident’s perspective, State Aid is funded through income and sales 
taxes.

Additional revenue sources include local receipts (Excise Tax, Meals Tax, etc.) and enterprise funds such as 
Sewer. Notably, Chapter 90 funding for municipal capital improvements has increased from $418,227 to 
$690,000 due to a five-year state-level bond bill. 
Operations 
Many Town Meeting articles relate to operational budgets. Lunenburg, like most municipalities in the 
Commonwealth, faces increased health insurance costs (14.84% increase), stagnant state aid, necessary salary 
increases to remain competitive, and inflation. As a result, the vast majority of department staffing plans have 
been maintained, placed on hold, or reduced. The Fire Department and School Department experienced the 
most significant impacts of these decisions this year, though all departments were affected. 
School Department Operations 
The School Committee and Superintendent have aligned their budget with the Town Manager’s plan, 
representing $25,787,474 in the Omnibus Budget. While grants augment this budget, Town Meeting voters 
determine omnibus budget funding. This budget necessitates eliminating approximately 28 Full-Time 
Equivalents (39 employees) at the time of writing this report. 
A Citizens Petition article for $2,099,337 aims to restore level funding to FY25 operations. As of this report, the 
Finance Committee has not received details of this article. The Select Board voted unanimously to recommend 
disapproval of this article and further voted to not include the Proposition 2 ½ override on the Annual Town 
Election Warrant. The Finance Committee also voted unanimously to recommend disapproval of this article.  
As a Proposition 2 ½ override the article would require passage at Town Meeting and at an Election, therefor no 
path exists for this article. 
If approved, the Tax Rate would increase by approximately $0.89 per $1,000 of property value, translating to an 
49
===PAGE 54===
average annual increase of $449 for a single-family household valued at $504,937.  This is a 6.19% increase in 
property tax. 
If the override is rejected, Article 12 proposes a $500,000 Free Cash transfer to the School Department as a 
temporary measure to delay service cuts. The School Committee has not yet determined how these funds would 
be allocated, with further details expected at Annual Town Meeting. 
Fire Department Operations 
The Fire Department currently operates three shifts covering 24/7 town operations, leading to a 48-hour, six-
day-per-week schedule. Sundays are covered by call and per-diem staff, with 62% of coverage provided by 
career firefighters. Of the 197 Massachusetts Fire Departments providing 24/7 coverage, Lunenburg is one of 
only six operating with three shifts. Most departments use a four-shift model, making Lunenburg less 
competitive in hiring and retention. The shift expansion plan remains on hold for another year. 
Borrowing 
The town employs two types of borrowing: 
• Regular Debt, typically for smaller projects within the Tax Levy Limit.
• Excludable Debt, requiring Town Meeting and ballot approval, which does not permanently increase
taxes—once the debt is repaid, the tax rate decreases.
According to the Massachusetts Division of Local Services, “Municipal debt is an important tool for city and 
town governments to finance the purchase of expensive capital assets and to construct and maintain municipally 
owned infrastructure.  However, debt cannot become overburdensome to either the community’s property tax 
paying population or to the budget. Capital purchasing and/or improvements should ideally be planned with its 
priorities analyzed and reviewed by a capital planning committee seeking to coordinate community planning 
with debt affordability and aiming to present its recommendations to municipal leaders and to the public.” 
The Town policy states that: 
• Principal and Interest on Regular Debt remain below 4%.
• Excludable Debt remains below 11%.
• Combined debt remains below 14%.
This year, Regular Debt decreases by $393,881.14, with these funds being utilized for: 
1. FY26 Capital Program ($244,576)
2. Operating Budget Offsets ($149,305)
Two debt-f
unded projects are on the Warrant and Ballot: 
1. Marshall Park improvements ($13.8M via Excludable Debt)
2. Municipal Building renovations ($21.4M - $22.3M via Excludable Debt)
Marshall Park Improvements ($13.8M via Excludable Debt): Require full funding approval before pursuing 
grants exceeding $1.5M. The Parks Commission has been encouraged to reapply for prior high-scoring grants 
such as PARC and Land and Water that scored highly. The Parks Commission was successful in securing 
Grants in excess of $1.5M prior to the project being put on hold in 2024.   As presented for the Marshall Park 
project, the Tax Levy would increase by approximately $0.46 per $1,000 on top of the FY26 increase. The 
average single-family household has a valuation of $504,937 and would result in an increase of $231 per year in 
taxes. This is a 3.2% increase in property tax. 
Municipal Building Renovations ($21.4M - $22.3M via Excludable Debt): As presented for the Municipal 
Building project the Tax Levy would increase by approximately $0.57 per $1,000 on top of the FY26 increase. 
50
===PAGE 55===
The average single-family household has a valuation of $504,937 and would result in an increase of $288 per 
year in taxes. This is a 3.97% increase in property tax. 
The estimated tax impact for the Marshall Park and Municipal Building projects will be detailed at Town 
Meeting.  Both projects combined would remain under the Combined debt policy limit of 14%.  The combined 
projects would be around the 11% excludable debt policy limit.  
 Summary 
This year’s Town Meeting presents critical budgetary decisions for voters. The Finance Committee 
acknowledges the financial concerns of residents and appreciates the work done by town boards and 
commissions to prepare budgets and warrant articles. Ultimately, the decisions rest with those who participate 
in Town Meeting. 
The Finance Committee extends gratitude to Town Manager Jennifer Warren-Dyment for her rapid 
acclimation to Lunenburg’s finances. We also thank Ezequiel Ayala for his financial analysis, as well as 
Superintendent Dr. Kate Burnham for her service, wishing her well in retirement. We appreciate all 
department heads, municipal staff, and school employees for their dedication to the town.      
Lunenburg Finance Committee 
• Christopher Menard, Chair 
• Jean Russell, Vice-Chair 
• Thomas Gray, Clerk 
• Jay Simeone 
• Tanya Dall 
• Ana Lockwood 
• Michael Rupp 
 
  
“A library outranks any other one thing a community can do to benefit its people. It is a never failing spring in 
the desert.”        -Andrew Carnegie 
 
A Year of Stewardship, Renewal, and Community Connection 
 
As Library Director, it is my pleasure to present this year’s annual report on the activities of the Lunenburg 
Public Library. After years of serving rural communities, I can say with confidence that what we have 
accomplished together this year is something special. Our library continues to be a true gem in the town—not 
only a place of books, but a living, evolving institution that reflects care, creativity, and deep community 
investment. 
 
We continue to make large and small changes to the library in an effort to constantly improve the facility, our 
resources, and the value the library brings to the community. 
 
This year we made significant strides in improving the comfort and sustainability of our building. The 
installation of mini splits, along with essential HVAC repairs, has greatly enhanced efficiency and 
51
===PAGE 56===
responsiveness throughout the library. Although the HVAC is aging and maintenance needs continue to arise, 
we saw a drastic improvement in the system’s efficiency after a repair in August. 
 
The exterior of the library received much-needed attention, including painting and general maintenance made 
possible by American Rescue Plan act funding. This work not only preserves the integrity of the building but 
also reinforces the library’s presence as a well-cared-for and inviting landmark in our town. Similarly, the new 
library sign that was added at the end of FY24 to the façade received lighting fixtures as its final touch. 
 
Gardens, Grounds, and Stewardship 
 
The Proctor Memorial Garden once again has a fully functioning waterfall. Another project that was completed 
thanks to ARPA funding, this work restores the patio and its surrounds to a condition that fully supports 
mindful appreciation of our environment, quiet contemplation, and even energetic play for young library 
visitors. 
 
We are grateful to Charles LeTarte for the generous donation of two handcrafted bird houses. These charming 
additions enhance the garden spaces in the front of the library and reflect the library’s commitment to local 
wildlife and environmental awareness. 
 
We also embraced a “Leave Your Leaves” approach in designated garden areas to support luna moth habitats. 
Our three River Birch trees on the southeastern corner of the building could support luna moths over the winter 
if the leaves are allowed to remain on the ground around the trees. This simple but meaningful practice 
reinforces our objectives to educate and demonstrate acts that support our local ecosystems. 
 
Programs, Outreach, and Community Engagement 
 
We kicked off our year with our best ever Summer Reading Program. Almost 400 people registered their 
reading endeavors, while hundreds of others attended the programming events. The highlight even more than 
the high levels of participation this year was our summer lunch program, sponsored by Main Street Bank, 
during which we served almost 650 lunches to Summer Reading Program participants and other visitors to the 
library. Both the library and Main Street Bank are excited to continue this partnership during future Summer 
Reading Programs. 
 
During FY25, we also developed our focused outreach plan to guide our efforts to connect with underserved 
patrons and non-library users outside the library walls. Through visits to Farmers’ or Artisan Markets, schools, 
local businesses and apartment complexes, our goal is to find new avenues to deliver services to our 
community. I thoroughly believe that the library has something that could benefit each and EVERYONE in 
town, when they are aware of its availability. 
 
Spaces for Growth and Belonging 
 
If there is one accomplishment of the library this year to focus on, it’s the renovation of the Teen Room, which 
transformed the space into a vibrant, welcoming space designed with our teens in mind. The updated room 
supports study, creativity, and social connection, and clearly signals that young adults have a valued place in the 
library. Most importantly, it creates a safe, comfortable environment for teens to use library resources without 
unintentionally disrupting access for other groups. These changes were funded by ARPA money, and the 
furniture was purchased by our Friends of the Lunenburg Public Library. The Friends also offered additional 
funding to support contingency expenses for the construction project. Our Friends of the Lunenburg Public 
Library are unique for a library of our size in their capacity to provide continued advocacy and financial 
support, and they make it possible for us to respond thoughtfully to both planned improvements and unexpected 
needs. 
 
52
===PAGE 57===
I also want to acknowledge and thank two long-time Trustees who have stepped away from their leadership 
roles at the library. Donna Saiia served as Trustee for nine years. Richard Mailloux served for eighteen years. 
Librar
y Trust Funds 
The Libr
ary Trustees continue to steward the trust funds that have been given to the library; these 
funds increased by 7.6% to a total value of $104,808.21 as of June 27, 2025. The majority of these funds, 
$63,848.09 were held in a Balanced Growth and Income Account managed by the investment firm Edward 
Jones, $36,000, was deposited in an FDIC-insured, brokered CD and the remainder was held in a money market 
for liquidity purposes. No trust funds were spent during this fiscal year due to the availability of ample grant 
funding and generous donations from the Friends of the Library. 
These t
rust funds are to be used to finance special library projects or programs or to enhance and enrich the 
Library’s services as opportunities arise and in accordance with the library’s Strategic Plan. The Trustees 
currently have no concrete plan to spend trust funds, focusing on the growth and appreciation of capital. 
Librar
y Gifts

ary received two monetary donations directly during this fiscal year. A gift of $150 from the Lewin 
family, and a gift of $250 from Chris Harlow and Kate Casey. These funds were fully expended as part of a 
purchase of software licenses for the Library’s public computers. We thank the donors for their generosity!

Adult S
ervices Highlights 
Programm
ing  
Programs and events at the library have been an increasingly successful part of the Adult Services Department, 
and FY25 proved to be no different. This year, the library hosted 182 programs and meetings for adult patrons, 
with a total of 2,067 attendees for the year.    
Highlight
s of programs for FY25 include:  
• Increasing popularity of the quarterly puzzle swap events, averaging 60 attendees and 550-600 puzzles
at each swap. These are by far the most popular events we hold every quarter.
• Workshops and events led by local experts were also popular. One of our goals for FY25 was to partner
with more Lunenburg residents for localized programming at the library, and this effort far exceeded our
expectations for success.
o A local guitar instructor hosted six Saturday morning music clinics once a month that were open to
anyone. A total of 95 aspiring musicians came to these classes throughout the year.
o On a September evening, a blacksmith came to demonstrate his craft with a mobile forge in the
parking lot, drawing 35 people to this one event.
o An occupational therapist led several exercise classes for us again, with 189 people learning and
53
===PAGE 58===
practicing better movement for their bodies.  
o The library saw 28 people complete winter sowing projects led by a library volunteer and gardening 
guru in February.  
o We also partnered with the Lunenburg Business Association and hosted a Lunch & Learn seminar 
led by a local realtor that was attended by 12 people.  
o And perhaps one of our biggest successes was the first annual Winter Artisan Market 
on Sunday December 8th that drew large crowds into the library and showcased our local artists. 
Managed in collaboration with a local beekeeper and town organizer, it was an amazing display of 
community effort that we hope will become an annual tradition.   
• Our special “Craft & Chill” programs, on one Friday evening every month, showed significant promise 
in bringing in the 20–40-year-old group. These individuals are part of an elusive 
demographic, and engaging with this group will continue to be an ongoing target for the library.  
Outreach  
Formalizing the library’s outreach services was another major focus for FY25. The Director, Assistant Director, 
and Digital Services & Outreach Librarian worked together to outline a plan to deliver library services outside 
the walls of the library. Our initial outreach visits in 2024-2025 were successful, with six offsite events attended 
and over 120 visitor engagements. Looking forward to 2025-2026, we will work to maximize the effectiveness 
of our visits based on the work we did this year on our Outreach Plan.   
 
Collections & Digital Services  
Incorporating feedback from various sources, we expanded part of our adult collection with the addition of new 
Memory Care Kits in autumn 2024. Curated for adults who are suffering from memory loss conditions and their 
caregivers, these kits circulate at the library and are available to anyone with an active library 
card.  Additionally, we added 13 new items to our Library of Things collection, including some backyard games 
like bocce, cornhole, and ring toss. Gaining popularity, this collection continues to grow and meet the needs of 
the community for items beyond typical library holdings.   
 
We have also started offering genre guide bookmarks in our fiction collections to help patrons find more books 
in the styles they like to read. This tool improves the browsing experience by giving examples of titles readers 
might like based on similar themes or styles.     
 
On the digital side of collections, this year we added two new resources and already see a 
high level of engagement: Hoopla and Freegal. We have 71 digital resources funded by CWMARS, our 
library, the Friends of the Library, and the state board of library commissioners that patrons can access with a 
library card. Our top used resources - Libby, Hoopla, Kanopy, and Freegal - offer patrons downloadable or 
streaming content like eBooks, eAudiobooks, music, movies and more.   
 
FY26 Goals for Adult Services:  
 
• Improve community connections with key prospective partners through targeted outreach efforts 
outlined in our FY25 Outreach Plan.  
• Develop a homebound delivery service to meet the needs of potential patrons with mobility or 
transportation obstacles.  
• Refine program planning to create a cohesive slate of events that meet a variety of interests 
while maintaining an appropriate balance of resources in the Adult Services department.   
• Identify other areas for growth in the adult collections through opportunities like new Library of Things 
54
===PAGE 59===
acquisitions, memory care kit expansions, digital resources, or entirely new collection development 
ideas to better serve adults in Lunenburg.  
Young Adult Services Highlights 
 
• The Teen Librarian offered 48 tween- and teen-focused events with a total of 285 participants.  
• 57 teens signed up for summer reading with 73,172 minutes of reading logged.  
• In an effort to support the increasing numbers of homeschooling families a series of 14 Homeschool 
Family Meet-up and Homeschool Craft Corner events brought in 206 participants.   
• Partnerships with the Teen Advisory Board and the Shapiro Educational and Behavioral Consultants 
created regular engagements and contributed positive events for teens.

• Increase Teen program participation by at least 10%.  
• Improve YA book recommendation experience. 
• Increase volunteer opportunities with the Teen Advisory Board 
• Expand partnerships with community groups and leaders to improve support for the teens in the 
community. 
Children’s Services Department Highlights 
 
• We started off the year with our best ever participation in our Summer Reading Program with a total of 
229 children regististering their participation.  These readers completed a total of 222,000 minutes of 
reading during our 6 week program!  
• We served a total of 640 free lunches during July. Sponsored by Main Street Bank, we offered food four 
days each week.  Generous portions of delicious and nutritious food were prepared and 
delivered by Employment Options, Inc. 
• Between 32 meetings of Little Listeners Story Times (ages 0-3) and 34 Preschool Story Times (ages 3-
5), more than 1,761 children visited the library. More than 200 visitors came to attend 8 STEM (Science, 
Technology, Education, Math) programs. More than 250 attended craft programs with Miss Joslyn. 
• 28 STEM education kits were available to borrow and these circulated a total of 75 times  
• Our focus in collection development was to improve the variety and volume of our graphic novel 
collection, which are important books to support the interest and reading development of school-aged 
children. This collection was shifted to a more central location to ease access and improve awareness. 
The 820 items in this collection circulated more than 5,000 times in FY25. 
• We continue to see that Library visits from young patrons and their families have definitely increased. 
Lots of children stop by almost every afternoon to socialize, participate in our passive and active 
programs, and check out their next books to read. 
 
GOALS FOR FY26  
 
• Increase participation of active library users and programs attendees.

PUBLIC ACCESS CABLE (PAC) 
Public Access Cable Committee (PACC) 
Steve Walker, Chair 
Faith Beall, Vice Chair 
Steve Archambault, Secretary 
Pete Lincoln, Member 
Paul Sarapas, Member 
Brian Vienneau, Member 
The Lunenburg Public Access Cable Committee (PACC) is responsible for the long-term plan to manage the 
Public Access Channels available to the Lunenburg community through Comcast, the town’s only cable-
service provider. 
The overall strategy includes providing and improving equipment to acquire, record, broadcast and re-
broadcast programming.  Original content is also conceived and produced by PAC.  Town events/meetings 
and school activities, as well as outside sources of programming, are all provided to increase the viewer’s 
knowledge of what is happening around town. 
56
===PAGE 61===
Two members of PACC resigned during FY25; Paul Sarapas and Pete Lincoln, leaving two vacancies on the 
committee.  Brian Vienneau joined the committee as the fourth member at the end of FY25.  There is one 
vacancy remaining. 
 
Programming 
 
CH8 – Government 
Most content on the Government Channel (Ch8) is locally broadcasted meetings.  In addition to several live 
weekly municipal meetings and re-broadcasts, other government programs downloaded from MassAccess 
MMX (massaccess.telvue.com), include Massachusetts State Agency PSA’s and programs of a political 
nature.   
 
There was an increase in local government meetings being recorded and uploaded to the government and 
YouTube channels, including the newly formed Trust Funds Committee, School Committee’s Superintendent 
Interview special meetings, Council on Aging, Stormwater Task Force, Economic Development Committee 
and special forums with the Master Plan Steering Committee. 
 
PAC produced “Talk of the Town” program in FY25, which is an interview program featuring the Town 
Manager.  This show airs on both the Government channel (Channel 8) and the Public Channel (Channel9).  
“Talk of the Town” is in the process of being revamped for FY26. 
 
Government meetings are recorded at the request of board/committee members and reviewed by PAC.  PAC 
makes every effort to accommodate requests based on availability.  Meetings are recorded and scheduled to 
rebroadcast on Ch8 and uploaded to the PAC’s YouTube Channel.  These YouTube meetings can be watched 
and/or downloaded at the convenience of viewers.  The number of rebroadcast meetings on Ch8 is over 150 
hours per month.  Rebroadcast meeting hours fluctuate depending on the time of year and number of meetings.   
 
CH9 – Public 
 
Public Access Cable (PAC) produced 75% of the local programming on Ch9 in FY25, down 3% from FY24.  
The total number of Public (Ch9) programs, both local and AccessShare (outside resources), decreased from 
125 in FY24 to 80 programs in FY25 resulting in a decrease of overall programs of 36 %.  This decrease in 
Ch9 programs was due to the growth of government recordings, a reduction of videographers and the transfer 
of LHS sports from Public Channel (Ch9) to the Educational Channel (Ch22). 
 
Sixty (75%) local programs included presentations, band concerts and featured projects. The remaining 20 
(25%) programs were obtained through shared media sources. 
 
 
 
Several categories of local programming were aired on Public Ch9. Among the top programs were 
Informational (31%), Events (25%), and Entertainment (32%), making up the majority of Ch9 programming 
(see chart below).

CH22 – Educational 
 
In May of FY20, PAC added an Educational Channel, Comcast Channel 22.  The additional channel has 
allowed PAC to feature local educational content to viewers in addition to the bi-monthly School Committee 
meetings.   
 
Many programs are shared between Channel 9 (Public) and Channel 22.  As of FY25, most of the channel’s 
content remains to be LHS sports, school events and concerts, as well as, downloaded resources such as 
“Democracy NOW” and “Kim’s Art Show.”  Other shows added to the channel have an educational or 
entertainment component to them, including “Wicked Good Food” and “Health Matters.” 
 
While it is optimal to receive programming from the local schools, PAC frequently subsidizes local 
programming with imported resources.  School Committee meetings, shared between Gov’t. (Ch8) and 
Educational (Ch22), airs frequently for the viewers convenience.  Other school events are recorded, often with 
the use of multiple cameras, as they occur throughout the year. 
  
“Lunenburg Storytime – The Books We Love” produced by PAC airs twice daily on Ch22 as an anchor of this 
channel.  Storytime has experienced tremendous popularity amongst residents as well as other media centers 
on the MMX database.  Community volunteers read children’s books in studio recorded monthly for 
broadcast.   
 
PAC continues to work toward content growth on all channels.
