Studio an
d Equipment 
PAC has
 had studio space in the TC Passios school since 2013.  We encourage the public to take advantage of 
the facilities and equipment offered for creative productions. The PAC manager also supports various town 
departments in the use of public access’ resources for their communication needs. 
In FY25 P
AC completed the upgrade of the audio system in the Bilotta Room at Town Hall with a Symetrix 
system.  This improvement was paid for through ARPA funding.  The speaker system was replaced and the 
control room upgraded with a tablet-based control panel for audio administration. 
The audio quality now experienced at meetings has been remarkable and has exceeded expectations.  
The studio has continued to experience the benefits of the FY24 upgrades, including the new switcher and 
cameras.  In FY25, PAC has added more lighting kits, portable wireless microphones and new and reused 
furniture for the set.  Many organizations have taken advantage of the studio using it for purposes such as 
event announcements. 
Staff 
In 
FY25, there was one full-time Manager, a Production Technician, 5 part-time videographers and a part-time 
contracted Bulletin Administrator.  One videographer resigned, leaving 2 vacancies in the organization. One 
videographer was hired to fill the open position at the end of FY25, leaving one open videographer position 
for FY26.  
The PAC Manager administers the day-to-day operation of the organization, including all production and 
programming schedules, staff scheduling, equipment and services procurement, social media and equipment 
and system troubleshooting.  Videographers provide video recording of government meetings and are 
available for local video assignments and projects.   
The Production Technician manages the day-to-day operations of the studio and assists the videographers with 
training and equipment needs.  Reporting to the Manager, he provides technical assistance for production and 
studio support.  The team relies on the Production Technician to oversee and assist with troubleshooting issues 
that arise.  He also advises on new equipment and supervises set up and administration of large productions. 
PAC’s 5 Videographers work 2-10 hours per week recording board and committee meetings, sports, events 
and special projects.  They work “in the field” as well as in the studio producing such shows as “Talkin’ 
Baseball” and “Movie Talk.”  The staff responds to recording requests from town officials and management on 
59
===PAGE 64===
a regular basis and help cover larger productions such as the LHS home football games and Town Meetings, 
resulting in high quality 4 camera productions. 
 
Videographer coverage has been entered in Google Calendar since FY25, which streamlines the scheduling 
process.  A calendar link is sent to the staff at the end of the month listing their upcoming assignments and is 
available for all videographers to access.  Dates for coverage are assigned and automatic reminders are sent 
out 2 days before an event to the videographer’s email.   
 
PAC is always looking for producers.  If a community member has a special skill or interest, PAC will help 
make the show happen.  Our studio and staff are equipped to assist new producers in video production.

FY25 from Comcast was $180,792 a decrease from the previous year’s $ 192,954 (6.3% reduction).  The 
budget starting balance was set at $208,772 for FY25 projected expenses and capital costs.

Summary 
P
AC experienced a significant decrease in Public Channel (Ch9) growth in FY25.  There were several 
reasons for the decline, including videographer’s hours and an increase in government meetings.  LHS 
sports were moved from Ch9 to the Educational Channel (Ch22) for the year, resulting in the decrease of 
public programming.  However, with careful planning and full videographer staff, PAC’s goal is to surpass 
FY25 growth in FY26. 
PAC offers more informational, entertainment and event category programs to the community.  These 
programs are popular on our stations, as well as on the Lunenburg Access YouTube channel.  Viewers are 
able to access a wide variety of shows, including concerts and LHS sports.  Content has been created with 
minimal staff, as videographers juggled multiple commitments to deliver local programming. 
While our shared PAC programming on MassAccess’ MMX database steadily grew in popularity, PAC’s 
local programming experienced a declined.  The MMX database content uploaded for sharing, include our 
popular featured PAC produced programs.  These shows are a strong representation of PAC’s ability to 
create quality content in the upgraded studio. The Educational Channel (Ch22) programming remains at a 
consistent rate of growth, while Government (Ch8) has increased with the addition of committee/board 
meetings.     
PAC staff is the core of the group.  The team is supportive and dedicated to the goals of the organization.  
Videographers are always learning new skills and training on new equipment.  They also continually cover 
hybrid board and committee meetings as requested without hesitation.  There are currently over 20 board 
and committee meetings that are recorded either through Zoom or in hybrid mode.  While often short 
staffed, PAC videographers have managed to keep up with government recordings and local shows of 
interest.

SENIOR PROPERTY TAX WORK-OFF PROGRAM 
JANUARY 1, 2025 – NOVEMBER 1, 2025 
 
The Senior Property Tax Work-Off Committee is administered by the Assistant Town Manager/HR Director. 
 
What is the Senior Property Tax Work-Off? 
 
The Senior Property Tax Work-Off Program offers income eligible senior citizens age 60+ an opportunity to 
have an abatement applied to their property tax bill. Participants work in various town departments doing jobs 
such as general office work, custodial, library shelving, meals on wheels, school assistance, etc., to earn the tax 
abatement. 
 
The 2025 tax abatement maximum is $1,500 and applied to the participant’s 3rd quarter real estate tax bill. 
 
The current hourly rate is $15.00 per hour. 
 
Eligibility Requirements 
 
· Applicants must be 60 years of age or older 
· Must occupy and be the owner of the property as of July 1st 
· Receive a property tax bill in their name or their spouse’s name (60 years+) 
· Meet the income guidelines of (proof of income is required): 
     one person household - $50,000  
     two person household - $75,000 
 · All new applicants are required to have a CORI background check 
 · Earnings are exempt from state taxes 
· Earnings are subject to OBRA if participants are not receiving a pension from either    
  Worcester Regional Retirement or Massachusetts State Teachers Retirement 
· Final amount earned will be applied to the 3rd quarter real estate tax  
· All Property Tax Workers must re-apply and be accepted annually for participation in the  
  program 
  
62
===PAGE 67===
2025 Participation 
 
During 2025, there were six residents who participated in the Program in the following departments: 
· Council on Aging 
· Public Library                
· Tax Collector 
· Assessors                                               
· Sewer 
 
These individuals as a whole, accumulated 293.75 hours amounting to $4,406.25, before deductions, in real 
estate tax abatements.  
  
 
SEWER COMMISSION 
 
The Sewer Commission bears the responsibility of overseeing the Sewer Enterprise Fund, planning for the 
future, and developing and implementing rules, regulations, and policies. Under the Town’s Charter, 
responsibility for the day-to-day operation, care and maintenance of the sanitary sewers are under the 
supervision of the Town Manager who delegates the responsibility to manage and maintain the operational side 
of the system to the Department of Public Works’ Director. The Town of Lunenburg does not operate a sewer 
treatment plant. Approximately 89% of Lunenburg sewer is discharged to the City of Leominster, and the other 
11% is discharged to the City of Fitchburg. 
 
The Sewer Commission started FY25 with the following members: John Reynolds-Chair, Brett Ramsden-Vice 
Chair, and Mike Mackin as Clerk.  At the end of August, John Reynolds stepped down from the Commission, 
leaving only 2 members. A mailing was sent out to all sewer customers, seeking new members. Four men 
applied and three new commissioners started on 9/24/24. With a full board, the Commission reorganized with 
Mike Mackin (Chair), Brett Ramsden (Vice Chair), Scott Dall (Clerk) and members Joe Hurley and Eli Torres. 
In early 2025 Mike Mackin and Eli Torres left the Commission but were replaced in May by Dan Proctor and 
Arthur Katis.   
 
At the end of August 2024, Paula Bertram resigned as Sewer Business Manager and it was several months 
before a replacement was found.  In the meantime, DPW Director Bill Bernard stepped in as acting Business 
Manager, in addition to running the DPW, and Paula consulted part-time assisting Bill with quarterly billing 
plus other tasks.  Andrew Valliere was hired in January 2025 and quickly learned the job with some training 
from Paula.  During all this transition, there were still multiple issues with doing the billing on Munis.  Outside 
consultants were brought in to enable the bills to go out relatively on time.  To Andrew’s credit, he persevered 
through some tough billing cycles and found ways to deal with the Munis issues. In May 2025, Susan Sullivan 
was hired as Assistant Sewer Business Manager.     
 
Currently there are 1,226 properties connected to the Lunenburg system and another 270 which have access to 
Town Sewer but are not yet connected. During FY25, 14 new accounts connected to Town Sewer. The 
Commission billed out $1,187,341.09 in quarterly sewer bills and $67,187.77 went to owners’ December 2024 
FY25 tax bills for non-payment of sewer charges. The delinquency rate has been averaging 18% and continues 
to be an issue.  
 
The Commission received $100,000 in ARPA funds for repairs to manholes that are allowing clean water to 
enter the system as I/I (Inflow & Infiltration).  The Manhole Repair IFB went out in September 2024 and the 
contract was awarded to Insituform Technologies for replacement of frames and covers, along with repairs to 
the inside of some manholes.  Due to various delays, work was scheduled to begin in July 2025.   
 
Major repair projects were completed in FY25 at the Mass 1 and Dana Street pump stations by our maintenance 
63
===PAGE 68===
contractor (CTE) and sub-contractor Pride Environmental.  The repair at Mass 1 involved replacing a rotting 
pipe under the station, repairing suction lines and replacing the gate valves at a cost of $98,000. The work was 
completed in November 2024. The Dana Street station is the main pump station that most of the waste goes 
through. It also had some rotting pipes that needed replacement similar to Mass 1 and the project was scheduled 
for the end of June 2025 at an estimated cost of $123,600.  As the pump stations are approaching 20 years of 
use, these projects will protect the stations from very costly emergency repairs.   
 
As in FY24, clogs in the pump stations continued to be a major concern. The Commission sent out reminders to 
users and placed notices in the Lunenburg Ledger noting how important it is to only flush waste and toilet paper 
down the system. Clogs jam the pumps and can cause costly repairs, which can impact sewer costs.  
 
At the 5/3/25 Annual Town Meeting, the Commission put forward 4 articles. The FY26 budget article 
appropriated $1,886,150.22 from anticipated Sewer Enterprise receipts of $1,583,917.45 and $302,232.77 from 
Retained Earnings to balance the budget. The second article transferred monies to the Capital Reserve 
Stabilization Fund in the amount of $5,133.15. The third warrant article transferred $19,104.40 into the Sewer 
Inflow/Infiltration Stabilization Fund used for mitigating I/I from entering the system. The fourth article 
transferred the sum of $1,545.00 into the OPEB Sewer Trust Fund. All four articles passed at Town Meeting. 
 
Prior to the end of FY25, the Commission discussed drawing up guidelines and a fee structure for proposed 
ADUs (Accessory Dwelling Units). Many of these units are being proposed and the Commission will be 
working with Town Counsel to incorporate changes into the by-laws in FY26.   
 
Sewer Commission as of report submittal (Feb 2026): 
Brett Ramsden, Chair 
Scott Dall, Vice-Chair 
Dan Proctor, Clerk 
Arthur Katis, Member 
 
 
VETERANS’ SERVICES 
 
Massachusetts leads the way in taking care of Veterans because only in MA, is there a Veterans Service 
Officers in every Town, either Full Time (Population over 12,000) or Part Time.   
 
Also, MA is the only State that offers Chapter 115 Benefits to its Veterans, Veterans families, or widows who 
qualify, can be provided this monthly financial assistance, which are reimbursements for specific living 
expenses such as heating fuel, medical care, and pharmacy co-payments.   
 
This Veteran Agent is State Certified by the Department of Veterans Affairs, so this Veteran Agent is an 
authorized representative, to process and file VA claims for Veterans benefits. 
 
Some other examples of Veterans’ Service Office assistance services include: 
 
--Assistance obtaining military service records 
--Housing assistance, including Veterans Shelters and Veterans Homes  
--Payments of “Welcome Home” bonuses for deployed Veterans  
--Assistance obtaining certain Massachusetts automobile and motorcycle license plates  
--Employment assistance  
--Property tax exemptions  
--Tax Exempt State Annuities for parents or spouses of those killed in Action or those who are 100% disabled.  
64
===PAGE 69===
--Burial Assistance   
 
During FY25 (July 1, 2024- June 30, 2025), Lunenburg started serving 5 recipients on Chapter 115 Benefits and 
ended with serving 7 recipients (Veterans, Families, or Widows) per month. Lunenburg paid out $50,323.27 in 
benefits, which is on average $4,193.61 per month. Lunenburg was reimbursed $38,283.61 by State, which is 
the maximum allowable 75% share. 
 
This Veteran Agent is located at the Ritter Building, 960 Massachusetts Ave. Office hours are Thursdays, 9:00 
AM thru 12:00 PM.  
 
If you are a Veteran, Family of a Veteran or know a Veteran, Please contact me at:  
mdurkee@Lunenburgma.gov, or 978-400-1938.  
 
Thank you,  
Michelle Durkee

BOARD OF HEALTH 
 
The Board of Health Office is located in the old Ritter Library building on the corner of Mass. Ave and 
Leominster Road.  Our office is on the upper level at the end of the hall on the right.  We can be reached at 978-
345-4146, extension 430.  
 
The Board of Health, with the assistance of its Agent, the Nashoba Associated Boards of Health, continued its 
mission to maintain its focus on public health and safety issues during 2025, reviewing Title  V Inspection 
Reports, building permits, septic and well permits, nuisance investigations, housing inspections, complaints and 
general public health related issues as well as resident concerns.    
 
In addition, our local Board continued to be tasked with responding to emerging health responsibilities.  The Board 
of Health assisted residents and businesses in understanding ever changing guidance and compliance with those 
orders.  We continued to conducted compliance checks and responded to complaints.  Through Nashoba Associated 
Boards of Health, vaccine clinics are held.   
 
Homeland Security and Emergency Preparedness P lanning is ongoing.  Meetings continue between our agents, 
other Town Departments and regional entities to develop a plan for not only Lunenburg but with our partner 
towns in our region.   
 
This year, we processed applications for Septic Permit Lot Tests, permits for new construction, new wells, 
repairs or upgrades to septic systems, well permits and food service permits.  The Board continues its vigilance 
with regard to septic systems, wells, food service inspections, housing and nuisance complaints and its general 
mandate to protect the environment and groundwater of the Commonwealth.   
 
The Board of Health provides numerous other services in cooperation with our Health Agents, the Nashoba 
Associated Boards of Health. This year, clinics provided our residents with inoculations for Flu as well as 
Senior well adult clinics.  
 
The Town of Lunenburg participates in the Central Mass. Mosquito Control Project.  Mosquito Control 
monitors the mosquito population in Lunenburg and advises of any potential diseases such as EEE and West 
Nile Virus and sprays frequently to control the population during the warmer times of the year.  Residents who 
want to have their property sprayed can do so by emailing to:  cmmcp@cmmcp.org , calling (508) 393-3055, 
completing a request on their webpage at:  http://www.cmmcp.org, or calling the Board of Health office.  This 
service is free of charge to residents. 
 
Please contact our office in the Ritter Building for more information.     
 
Please remember that our Board Members volunteer their time and efforts on behalf of you, the Citizens of 
Lunenburg. If you have something that you think we can help you with, we are at your service. Contact the 
Board office at 582-4146 ext. 430.    
 
 
NASHOBA ASSOCIATED BOARDS OF HEALTH 
 
Nashoba Associated Boards of Health continues to serve the Lunenburg Board of Health in meeting the challenges 
of environmental and public health needs in your community.  
 
In 2025, the Nashoba Associated Boards of Health (NABH) continued to advance regional public health through 
the ongoing support of two key grants: the Public Health Excellence (PHE) Grant and the Contact Tracing and 
Case Investigation (CT/CI) Grant.  
 
67
===PAGE 72===
The PHE Grant has enabled the onboarding of staff over time, including two Health Agents, an 
Education/Communication Specialist, and part- time staff to help with food inspections and manage the grant; the 
CT/CI Grant has further supported NABH by funding a full-time Public Health Nurse.  Staff provided by these 
grants have enhanced NABH’s ability to provide inspections, community outreach, and health education, fostering 
a more robust and adaptable public health infrastructure across participating municipalities.  
 
In response to the closure of the Nashoba Valley Health Center and the Massachusetts Department of Public 
Health’s new public health services delivery model, Foundational Public Health Service (FPHS), the Association 
contracted for a Community Health Needs Assessment (CHNA) to better understand the needs of our community; 
the contractor delivered their report at the end of December. The work was made possible by the CT/CI Grant. 
 
Together, these grants have strengthened NABH’s ability to meet evolving community needs, improving disease 
surveillance, emergency preparedness, and equitable access to high-quality public health services. Through a 
collaborative and resource-sharing approach, NABH has built a resilient and modern public health system that 
continues to effectively serve all member communities.  
 
Environmental Health Department 
 
The Nashoba Sanitarian serving your community is always available at our Ayer office and has regular hours in the 
Town Hall to serve your residents and work with your Town Hall staff. 
 
Food Service Licensing and Inspections (64)        
This office licenses, inspects, responds to complaints and conducts follow-up on concerns in licensed operations.  
Our staff review plans for new/renovated facilities to ensure they are constructed in accordance with the Food 
Code. 
 
Recreational Permitting and Inspections (11)       
This office licenses, inspects and responds to complaints regarding recreational camps for children, bathing beaches 
and public and semi-public pools.  We sample bathing beaches weekly, providing the results to community partners to 
ensure swimming only occurs at the public and semi-public beaches which meet State regulations. 
 
Housing & Nuisance Investigation (27)        
This office inspects dwelling units for compliance with the State Sanitary Code upon complaints, and prior to 
occupancy.  We issue orders to affect corrective actions, reinspect, and initiate legal action if necessary.  Similarly, 
complaints from residents concerning unsanitary conditions, environmental concerns and/or pollution are 
investigated and actions taken as required. 
 
Title 5 related work – On-site Sewage Disposal 
We received 83  applications for soil testing (testing necessary for the design of a sewage disposal system) and 
witnessed 158 tests performed (perc tests and soil evaluation holes). We have received 49 applications for sewage 
disposal systems and reviewed 32 plans for new and replacement systems.       
Our office has prepared 34 sewage disposal system permits for the Board’s review and approval: 15 for new 
construction and 19 for the replacement of failed systems. We conducted 92 inspections during the construction of 
sewage disposal systems. 
 
We provide consultation to your residents on matters relating to sewage disposal systems (operations, inspection 
and expansion (including the proposed accessory dwelling units (ADU)).  As part of this work, we reviewed 86  
Title 5 inspection reports. 
 
Private Wells 
We reviewed water quality and quantity reports and interpreted the results for your residents.  As water quality 
awareness has increased, we have assisted communities with systematic water testing programs (generally upon 
68
===PAGE 73===
property transfer); providing consultation and direction on how to comply with local requirements. 
 
Private wells are regulated by local regulation, and we issued 17 permits for new and replacement private water 
supply wells.     
 
Rabies Clinics 
The number of residents attending our community clinics has dropped.  Understanding the importance of service, 
we have offered regional clinics to ensure convenient access to a clinic.   
   
Community Health Nursing 
  
Nashoba’s Community Health Nursing program provides an essential public health service to its member 
communities guided by the American Nurses Association (ANA) Standards of Public Nursing Practice.  Our 
nursing team underwent significant changes this year, a retirement and staff moving to new positions.  As of the 
end of 2025 the nursing team has been rebuilt, and we are working quickly to restore services while remaining 
adaptable to changes requested by our member communities and to meet the obligations outlined in the FPHS. 
Town Nurse Hours were “on pause” July through December 2025 while we re-built our public health nursing 
team. During this time, the core, State-mandated communicable disease case investigation and reporting 
continued. 
The Nashoba nursing team reviewed, investigated and reported on all mandated communicable and infectious 
diseases (including respiratory diseases) while working closely with the Massachusetts Department of Public 
Health’s (MDPH) epidemiology team. Though this daily reporting and investigation work is not always visible, 
it is vital to protect the public from the spread of disease and includes: 
• Working with MDPH and local Boards of Health to identify and manage clusters of disease/exposures in 
the district.  
• Working with school nurses and childcare providers in the district to provide information, offer advice 
and support. 
• In conjunction with our Health Agents, we work with individuals in challenging living conditions 
(unsanitary, hoarded) to facilitate services to mitigate situations which would allow an individual to 
remain in their home. 
• We work with Councils on Aging, community outreach workers and other staff in town to help residents 
find services they need. 
Communicable Disease Number of Cases investigated: 
 
 Calicivirus/Norovirus .................................................. 5 
 Hepatitis C ................................................................... 1 
 Human Granulocytic Anaplasmosis ........................... 4 
 Influenza ...................................................................... 136 
 Novel Coronavirus (SARS, MERS, etc) .................... 59 
 Salmonellosis .............................................................. 1 
 Streptococcus pneumoniae ......................................... 1 
 
The Nashoba nursing staff provided monitoring and guidance to travelers and residents testing positive for TB 
infection (not contagious) throughout the district this calendar year and have also been managing 2 active TB 
cases in the District during 2025.   The towns in the Nashoba District fall into a LOW RISK TB category (as 
defined by MDPH). 
 
In addition to the mandated disease surveillance and reporting, our staff serves as a resource to the community, 
69
===PAGE 74===
addressing questions and concerns from the public on a variety of health and safety issues (immunizations, head 
lice, chronic disease management, safe drug/sharps disposal etc.) in a variety of ways including:  
• Monthly town nurse hours were offered from January through June 2025 and were “paused” July 
through December while we rebuilt our Public Health Nursing team. Nashoba nursing staff conducted 
town nurse hour events this year, including 6 in Lunenburg (including the regional flu clinic). Town 
nurse hours include an opportunity for blood pressure checks, diet/nutrition information and teaching, 
medication review, and assistance with identifying and coordinating community resources and referral 
as needed.  
• In 2025 we partnered with local pharmacies to offer our seasonal vaccine clinics, expanding vaccine 
availability with COVID and RSV vaccines in addition to influenza and preserving local public health 
funding and capacity.  In collaboration with these pharmacies, we conducted 11 vaccine clinics 
throughout the district vaccinating 1019 attendees and providing 1,498 vaccines.  
• The nurses are available to make home visits to make general assessments, assist with coordinating care 
and services, provide support/teaching and make referrals to other local community resources.  Our 
nurses made twenty-one home visits and received twenty-eight resident calls. 
• Nashoba has offered community outreach and educational programs covering topics such as disease 
prevention, chronic disease management, emergency preparedness, mental health awareness, and overall 
wellness.  We continued to offer grief support group availability. 
• Nashoba staff attend and regularly participate in collaborative meetings, trainings, and conferences to 
keep current with the latest developments in local public health.  
• Nashoba staff facilitated several meetings with key stakeholder groups to promote partnership, 
encourage strong regional communication, and foster working relationships.  This has helped to identify 
common goals and guide future public health initiatives and outreach. 
 
Community Health- Communications and Public Health Education Summary, 2025 
The Public Health Educator/Communications Specialist (PHE/CS) role has continued to evolve since its 
inception in Sept 2021 to best support the needs of the district. In the past year, the PHE/CS has been 
communicating timely and relevant public health information to towns and stakeholders and providing 
educational opportunities across the district with a social determinants of health (SDOH) lens.  This work has 
included: 
• Providing community educational opportunities to residents both independently and collaboratively with 
the PH nursing team based on community need- Harm reduction, emergency preparedness, positive 
aging, etc. 
• Serving as project lead for a community health assessment from June to December, integrating 
contractor management, stakeholder engagement, communications, marketing, and logistical planning 
• Partnering with CHNA9 and THFCM to launch a local Nashoba Health Equity Micro-Coalition 
supported by the NABH Shared Services Coordinator 
• Maintaining up-to-date distribution lists, communicating within the district via channels such as 
quarterly newsletters, regular email updates, etc. pertaining to public health news, information, and 
events such as vaccine and rabies clinics.  Continuing to explore and evaluate communication methods 
to reach as many community members as possible.   
• Maintaining social media presence to reach more people in district with current, relevant, and seasonal 
public health information.  Serving as a resource for community members with public health related 
questions across a broad range of topics 
• Overseeing and administering a grant and donation-funded community grief support program 
70
===PAGE 75===
• Offering and instructing adult mental health first aid certification courses for municipal staff, community 
volunteers, and other groups serving NABH region residents 
• Providing education and advocacy related to harm reduction, treatment and de-stigmatization of 
substance use disorder 
• Serving on the Governor’s Nashoba Valley Health Planning Working Group to identify data and 
community-driven local priorities for closing critical healthcare gaps 
• Organizing technical assistance and resource-sharing meetings for municipalities related to opioid 
abatement settlement funds, with a fully executed IMA between 7 towns realized in 2025 
• Collaborating with the new Shared Services Coordinator to maximize resources and identify 
opportunities for capacity building 
• Building and maintaining relationships with stakeholders through meetings and attending community 
events to represent NABH 
• Serving as the primary administrator for NABH’s annual vaccine clinics in our first year partnering with 
a pharmacy 
• Attending trainings with an emphasis on health equity and public health service regionalization.  
• PHE/CS has utilized an SDOH lens to identify health inequities that most impact the NABH region to 
focus efforts and collaborate with community partners to lessen these gaps. 
• Developing educational and communications materials for district and individual towns by request 
including print, electronic, presentation, and TV/news media materials.  Publishing the first NABH 
regional Annual Report document 
• Maintaining and improving upon NABH website to keep info relevant and accessible 
• Co-organizing events to facilitate the exchange of information such as a springtime Social Services 
Networking Fair 
 
FIRE DEPARTMENT 
 
The Firefighters and Emergency Medical Technicians of the Lunenburg Fire Department are dedicated to 
providing the highest quality, most cost-effective fire, and emergency medical services to the citizens of the town 
of Lunenburg. 
 
In 2025, your Fire Department responded to 2006 calls for service, which broke down as follows:  
 
Fires, Alarms & Hazardous Conditions        387 
Emergency Medical                                        1233 
Inspections 320 
Other 29 
 
This level of activity was similar to 2024, with a slight decrease in fires, alarms and hazardous conditions, but 
an increase in EMS responses.  The closure of Nashoba Valley Medical Center continues to strain the Region’s 
EMS Services.  Ambulances traveling to distant hospitals or “holding the wall” waiting for a bed locally have 
resulted in increased out of service times for all services in the area.  For our Department, this has resulted in a 
36% increase in mutual aid EMS responses and an average of 15 additional minutes in turnaround time for an 
ambulance to become available.  It is not uncommon for one of the ambulances to be “holding the wall” in the 
local Emergency Department for 20 minutes or more waiting for a bed. 
 
The Department consists of 10 Career and 30- 35 On Call/Per Diem Firefighters and Emergency Medical 
Technicians, plus a full -time Administrative Assistant. The Station is staffed by three Firefighter/EMT’s or 
Paramedics. This coverage is provided by a combination of our Career Staff and Per Diem Firefighters. The 
71
===PAGE 76===
greatest issue we continue to confront is having enough part time staff to cover the 96 hours of Per Diem 
coverage each week.  This is particularly true of having enough Part time Firefighter/Paramedics.  The addition 
of a 4
th shift of 3 Firefighter/Paramedics is critical to our future operations.  This would allow Career Staff to 
cover 7 days per week, not 6.  This would improve working conditions for our staff and decrease the amount of 
forced overtime we are experiencing.  The Per diem coverage could then be reallocated to assist with staffing 
our second ambulance for much of the week.  This is a concern that is coming to the forefront with increasing 
numbers of ambulance calls and longer transport and turnaround times at hospitals.  In 2025 we had to rely on 
mutual aid 65 times for a mutual aid ambulance.  This was due to either insufficient staffing to cover the second 
ambulance, an ambulance being out of service or both ambulances were already on calls. 
 
Staffing the Station with dual trained Fire/EMS personnel ensures that we are prepared to respond to whatever 
emergency occurs. As many calls require more personnel than are on duty, we continue to rely heavily on the 
dedication and commitment of our On Call Firefighters and Emergency Medical Technicians.   
 
The Department’s Paramedic Program is now in its third year.  Having this level of service in- house allows us 
to provide advanced level EMS care to our residents and not draw on resources from other communities.  Of the 
Staff on Duty, One-two are always Firefighter/Paramedics. Paramedic training is more than two thousand hours 
and requires anywhere from 9- 18 months of college level classroom education, clinical time, and field 
internships to obtain certification. Firefighter/Paramedics Andrew Bolton and Jenny Smith serve as our ALS 
and EMS Coordinators. 
 
Lieutenant Christos Lekaditis and Firefighter Carl Gamberdella make up the Department’s Fire Prevention 
Division and conducted 350 Inspections in 2025. This is in addition to multiple fire drills and public education 
initiatives. By law, we are required to inspect Smoke and Carbon Monoxide Detector installations when a home 
is sold. We are also required to inspect all new oil burners and propane gas installations. Any business holding a 
liquor license also requires a yearly inspection for fire safety issues. In addition, all new construction projects 
must have their plans reviewed for proper fire protection, as well as ongoing inspections during the construction 
process. Other Fire Safety Initiatives undertaken during 2025 include: 
 
- Continuation of a Department of Fire Services Grant in the amount of $6953 to assist with Fire Safety 
Education in both the schools and with Senior Citizens. This Grant provides funding for both materials 
and personnel to bring fire safety education to our most vulnerable populations. Firefighter Olivia Hayes 
serves as our Coordinator for this program. 
- Participation in the Senior Center’s “Sand for Seniors” Program. This program provides buckets of 
walkway sand for Senior Citizens who were unable to obtain it for themselves in the winter months. In 
partnership with this program, Firefighter Olivia Hayes delivers the sand to the residents and when 
possible, inspects their homes for Smoke and Carbon Monoxide Detectors. If the resident was lacking 
detectors, Firefighter Hayes can install free detectors obtained through the Senior SAFE Grant.  
- It should be noted that each year, 50% of fatal fires are found to have nonfunctional or nonexistent 
smoke detectors. 
 
 Fire Prevention has become increasingly complex as codes change and new technologies appear. To keep pace, 
both Inspectors hold certifications with the State as Fire Prevention Officers - Level 1 or 2.  In addition two 
other members have also completed the Level one training and other staff have completed the Basic Level 
training. 
 
Four Firefighters from Lunenburg continue to be members of the Fire District 8 Technical Rescue and Dive 
Teams. These regional teams respond to high risk and specialized rescue situations, such as Confined Space, 
Rescue from Heights, Building and Trench Collapse, Swift/Floodwater and Underwater Search and Recovery. 
In conjunction with these Teams, Lunenburg operates both a Water Rescue Unit and a Collapse/Trench Rescue 
Trailer.  
 
72
===PAGE 77===
Our Apparatus fleet is currently in particularly good condition, with two pumpers and the Ladder truck at or 
under 10 years old. Engine 2, our 1989 GMS Brush Fire Truck, has exceeded its lifespan and is currently 
scheduled for replacement under the proposed FY ’27 Capital Plan.  Our Rescue Pumper, Squad 3, remains in 
good condition though it is nearing the end of its projected life span. This unit is scheduled for Replacement in 
FY’29. This unit is a dual-purpose Pumper and Rescue Unit, carrying the Jaws of Life, High Pressure Air Bags, 
and other specialized rescue tools. 
 
The Ambulances are experiencing increased wear and tear as we see not only more calls for service but longer 
transport times to more distant hospitals and critical care facilities. 35 years ago, we had four hospitals within a 
10–15-minute transport time. Today there is only one. In addition, many cardiac and trauma patients require 
definitive treatment in specialty facilities. This often requires transport to Worcester or occasionally even 
Boston.   Our new Rescue 1 arrived in the spring of 2025 and is in service as our Primary Ambulance.  Rescue 
2, a 2019 unit has been rotated to be the back up ambulance.  The former Rescue 1, a 2014 International has 
been rotated to replace our water rescue truck which was a 1998 ambulance. 
 
State and Federal Grants provided additional equipment to the Department including: 
- Drying Rack for drying firefighting clothing after washing/decontamination 
- Replaced broken/obsolete chain saws 
- New Forest Fire Helmets and gloves to replace ones over 20 years old 
- 2 Handheld Thermal Imaging Cameras 
 
As I write what will be my last Town Report before my retirement, I must reflect on the progress the Fire 
Department has made in the last 13 years.  When I assumed the role of Fire Chief in 2012, much of our front -
line apparatus was approaching or over 20 years old.  Our staffing only provided 2- 3 Firefighters during the 
day.  Our On Call staff responded to all calls after 6pm.   We relied on other services to provide Advanced Life 
Support for our ambulance patients.  Today nearly all our front -line apparatus has been updated and is 10 years 
or less in age.  We have 24/7 coverage at the Station and provide our own Advanced Life Support.  These 
improvements were possible due to the unwavering support of Town residents, Town Government and most of 
all the men and women of the Lunenburg Fire Department.  It is my fervent wish that this support continues to 
bring the Department over its next great hurdle.  The need for the 4
th Shift. 
 
In conclusion, I would like to thank the members of the Lunenburg Fire Department for their outstanding 
commitment to the Town. Their enthusiasm, professionalism and dedication are what truly make the 
Department the high- quality organization that it is.  I do not say it enough, but so many of our staff deserve 
accolades for the work they do “behind the scenes” every day to make the Department what it is. I cannot ask 
for a better group of men and women, and it is my profound honor to have served with them. 
 
I would also like to thank the other department heads, boards, and commissions for their continuing support 
during my tenure The level of inter-departmental cooperation that occurs daily is invisible to most people, but it 
continues to be critical to the smooth operation of the Town. Finally, my thanks also go out to the citizens of 
Lunenburg for their ongoing support of public safety. Such support allows us to continue providing the high 
level of service you have come to expect from us.

POLICE DEPARTMENT 
 
 
 
 
     
Mission Statement:  
We, the members of the Lunenburg Police Department, strive to provide the highest quality of police services to 
our community members and visitors alike.  We believe in Community Policing, and all the benefits that are 
realized from this concept.  We will strive to maintain the strong partnerships that have been developed with our 
community and continuously look for ways to improve that relationship.  We will strive to deliver police 
services in a fair and impartial manner, consistent with all applicable laws, while respecting all individual 
rights.  We will use innovative and modern ideas and techniques to solve crimes, apprehend criminal offenders, 
and help resolve quality of life issues.   
 
Values: 
• We are committed to ensuring that all of our actions reflect the utmost integrity and honesty.  
• We are committed to respecting individual rights, human dignity, and the values of all members of the 
community and members of the Lunenburg Police Department. 
• We are a proactive and problem-solving agency. 
• We strive to serve our citizens in a professional manner with compassion, respect, and empathy. 
• We are committed to providing the highest quality of police services to our community. 
• We recognize the value of our employees and recognize they are our most valued resource.  We will 
strive to provide our employees with the necessary resources, training, and career development to 
enhance their professionalism. 
• We recognize that the well-being of our employees is of the utmost importance.  We will strive to 
provide the necessary assistance to assure our employees enjoy a long and satisfying career.

Department Review: 
It is a privilege to present the Lunenburg Police Department’s Annual Report. In 2025, the Department 
continued to face challenges related to economic conditions, population growth, increased traffic and increase 
of cyber-crimes involving within the community. Ongoing nationwide difficulties in police recruitment and 
retention have also impacted the Department; however, progress has been made in addressing these challenges. 
Despite these obstacles, the Department remains proactive and fully committed to ensuring the safety of the 
community.  
 
Promotions:  
In October 2024, Chief Thomas Gammel retired, and Lieutenant Jeffrey Thibodeau was promoted to Interim 
Chief of Police. In November 2024, Sergeant Sean Zrate was promoted to Acting Lieutenant. Both positions 
later became permanent. In 2025, Interim Chief Thibodeau was formally appointed Chief of Police in April, and 
Acting Lieutenant Zrate was appointed to the rank of Lieutenant in August. 
 
New-Hire Updates: 
In May 2025, Officer Brody Tapply was appointed as a student officer and attended the Basic Recruit Academy 
at Fitchburg State University. Upon successfully completing the academy, he was sworn in as a full-time patrol 
officer in September 2025. 
 
In October 2025, the Department welcomed two additional officers, Officer Grace Kerr and Officer Elijah 
Weiss. Officer Kerr is a graduate of Fitchburg State University and previously served with the Fitchburg State 
University Police Department beginning in September 2023. Officer Weiss joined the Department from the 
Mendon Police Department, where he served since October 2023. Prior to that, he was employed by Fitchburg 
State University from 2021 through 2023. 
 
Programming Updates:  
Through a partnership with the Lancaster Police Department and Open Sky Community Services, the 
Department was able to secure funding to implement a Co-Response Model for mental health–related calls. As 
a result, Co-Response Clinician Vanessa Rivera began working with the Department in December. This 
program enhances our ability to respond effectively to mental health incidents and support community members 
during mental health related calls, as they continue to increase. The department has addressed the emerging 
nationwide trend of cyber-crimes and fraud. We have been fortunate to have highly trained and proactive 
Officers with the expertise and guidance of Officer Bradley McNamara in combatting these cyber-crime trends. 
Officer McNamara has had many successful prosecutions and seizure of illicit funds that lead to the victims 
recovering their financial loses. 
 
2025 Statistical Information: 
The overall goal of the Lunenburg Police Department is a reduction of crime, assure quality of life issues are 
addressed, and provide a safe community to live, work and visit.  The Department evaluates effectiveness, in 
part, by statistical information that is generated through the Records Management System.

Training: 
Training continues to be a priority for the Department. We obtained a department MILO advanced training 
system with earmark funding from the state. We now have the ability to train in virtual simulation-based 
programs. We have department instructors for specialized areas including Firearms, Less Than Lethal, 
Defensive Tactics, RAD, Active Shooter, MILO instructors, ALERRT &ALICE trainers and Resiliency 
(Officer Well Being).  The Department’s in-house trainers look for up to date ways to complement our annual 
MPTC state mandated in-service training for all officers.   
 In addition, many officers have attended specialized training in various areas of Policing as a part of the 
department’s professional development.  Training includes Cybercrimes, Accident reconstruction, Crisis 
Intervention Team, Critical Incident Management System, FBI-LEEDA, Servant Officer and Intentional 
77
===PAGE 82===
Leadership Programs.   
The Department also provides training designed for our non-sworn members, such as Police Executive 
Assistant Conference, and Massachusetts Public information requests training.     
 
 
Nashoba Valley Regional Dispatch District: 
The Nashoba Valley Regional Dispatch District (NVRDD) is a Regional Emergency Communications Center 
located in Devens, MA. NVRDD serves as the 911 public safety answering point and dispatch center for Police, 
Fire and EMS for the communities of Lunenburg, Berlin, Bolton, Boxborough, Havard, Lancaster, Devens, 
Hudson and Department of Defense at Devens.  NVRDD serves a total population of just over 55,000 residents 
and thousands more visitors to the orchards, farms, businesses, and attractions within its eight communities. 
 
Conclusion:  
I would like to thank the Citizens and businesses of the Lunenburg community for their continued support of the 
police department. Without collaboration with our community partners, we would not be successful.  We look 
forward to the New Year and continue to bring the very best in policing to our community. 
 
Most importantly I would like to thank all the employees and their families of the Lunenburg Police Department 
for their dedication to the Community, and the work that they perform daily. When working for a public service 
department the employees work twenty-four hours a day 365 days a year to keep our community safe, this is not 
an easy task for any Law Enforcement Officer and their families. The personnel of the Lunenburg Police 
Department play a vital role day to day in making the Town of Lunenburg a great and safe place to live, work 
and visit for all.   
 
 
Thank you,  
Chief Jeffrey Thibodeau

PLANNING BOARD 
Two (2) ANR Plans (land division plans not requiring approval under the Subdivision Control Law, but 
still requiring Board endorsement) were approved. 
Lunenburg’s Stormwater Bylaw requires the Board review and approval for any 
development/redevelopment project that disturbs one or more acre(s) of land. This Bylaw safeguards the 
public health, safety, welfare and the environment by minimizing adverse impacts of contaminated 
stormwater both off-site and downstream. These updates are more likely to affect large commercial 
projects and multi-family dwellings; not the average homeowner wanting to put in a shed, garden, pool, 
etc. The Board approved stormwater permits for the following. 
 
• Construction of 3 Single-Family homes, 171 Arbor St 
• Reconfigure parking, 494 Electric Ave 
• Construction of a single-family home, 70 Kilburn St 
• Construction of Single-Family home & Expand Common Driveway, 560 Goodrich St 
• Construction Single-Family home, 795 Leominster Rd 
• Construction Single-Family home, 791 Leominster Rd 
• Construction 5 Multi-Family homes, 213 Electric Ave 
• Construction Single-Family home, 255 Sunnyhill Rd 
• Construction 5 Single-Family homes on 5 ANR lots, 100 Townsend Harbor Rd 
 
Lunenburg has four designated Scenic Roads; Lancaster Avenue, Flat Hill Road, Northfield Road, and 
Leominster Road. As these are Scenic Roads, any repair, maintenance, re-construction, or paving work 
in the Town’s right-of-way cannot involve the cutting or removal of trees or the tearing down or 
destruction of stone walls without consent of the Planning Board and/or Tree Warden.
 
• Removal of large dying maple tree, 63 Lancaster Ave 
 
The Planning Board received and approved site plans for the following 
• Battery Energy and Storage System, 485 Leominster-Shirley Rd 
• 1092 SF addition, 110 Pleasant St 
• 10 Space gravel parking area, 801 Northfield Rd 
• 10 Space parking area, 994 Northfield Rd 
• Construction of quality air controlled building, 1260 Reservoir Rd 
• Change Use, 1335 Mass Ave 
• Construct a commercial building, 494 Electric Ave 
 
The Planning Board approved two (2) Site Plan Modifications. 
• Reduce project size, 260 Falulah Rd 
• Addition 10x12 Ft Shed, 230 Flat Hill Rd

ZONING BOARD OF APPEALS 
The Zoning Board of Appeals heard S everal petitions during the calendar  year 202 5. There were several 
applicants seeking special permits and special permits for dimensional variations. The Zoning Board meetings 
are held on the second and Fourth Wednesdays of the month at Town Hall at 7:00PM on an “as needed” basis 
or Zoom as an alternative.  
 
The Zoning Board consists of the following members: Alfred Gravelle, Chairman, James Besarkarski, Vice-
Chairman, Members: David Blatt, Patrick Callahan and Anthony Nicastro and Donald Gurney, Associate 
members. 
 
The functions of your Zoning Board of Appeals as required by statutes are as follows:  
 
1. To hear and decide applications for Variances from Town’s Protective By-Laws as authorized in the 
By-laws.  
2. To hear and decide applications for Special Permits as authorized in the Town’s Protective By-laws.  
3. To hear and decide appeals in accordance with Chapter 40A, Section 8 of the Massachusetts General Laws, 
as amended, from any person aggrieved by reason of his or her inability to obtain a permit or enforcement 
action from the Administrative Officer or Board in the Town, the Regional Planning Agency, abutting City 
or Town or other Administrative Official.  
4. To act upon application for comprehensive permits in accordance with Massachusetts General Laws, Chapter 
40B. The Zoning Board of Appeals reviews requests for comprehensive permits submitted to the Board 
under Chapter 40B by developers who wish to create affordable housing. These requests can, by law, 
circumvent local zoning ordinances.  
The office of the Zoning Board of Appeals is located in the Ritter Memorial Building, 960 Massachusetts 
Avenue, Lunenburg. Business hours are 8:00 AM to 4:00 PM Monday and Wednesday and 8:00 AM to 6:00 
PM Tuesday and Thursday, closed Friday. If you need any assistance from the Zoning Board of Appeals, please 
contact us at 978-582- 4146, ext. 421 during regular business hours. Zoning Board applications are available in 
the office and online at www.lunenburgma.gov

AD HOC OPEN SPACE ADVISORY COMMITTEE TO THE PLANNING BOARD 
 
The Open Space Committee’s efforts in 2025 principally focused on gaining a better understanding of the Town 
of Lunenburg’s portfolio of vacant, undeveloped, “general municipal” lands, and assessing which of those 
properties may be repurposed more specifically to meet needs identified in the Town’s Open Space and 
Recreation Plan.   
 
The Open Space Committee applied for and received an extension to the Open Space and Recreation Plan 
required by the state.  The revised plan is now due June 30, 2029. 
 
The Open Space Committee also reviewed several Chapter 61 withdrawal notifications this past year as part of 
the town’s defined multi-board process.  
 
The Open Space Committee has drafted a Town Bylaw for consideration at the May 2026 Town Meeting.  The 
Bylaw changes the status of the Open Space Committee from ad hoc committee of the Planning Board to a 
standing standalone committee.   
 
 
Respectfully submitted, 
Michael Toohill, Chair

LUNENBURG PUBLIC SCHOOLS  
Superintendent’s Message 
 
To the Citizens of Lunenburg, 
 
It is with profound joy and a deep sense of honor that I submit my first annual report as your Superintendent of 
Schools. As I conclude my first year in this role, I reflect on the warm welcome I have received from this 
community. I extend my deepest gratitude to the Town Manager, Town Department Heads, the Select Board, 
the Finance Committee, and the School Committee for their partnership and support. It is a privilege to serve a 
town that cares so deeply about its children. 
 
My tenure began with a commitment to listen. Before setting a new course, I needed to understand the heart of 
Lunenburg. Through three comprehensive surveys and countless conversations, I heard from 491 students, 106 
staff members, and 186 families. 
 
What I learned was powerful. I discovered a district with a strong heartbeat; where staff take immense pride in 
their colleagues and where families value the safety and opportunities our schools provide. However, I also 
heard clear calls for growth. While 70% of our students feel supported, we learned that a sense of belonging is 
fragile; too many students told us they do not yet feel fully accepted for who they are. Families expressed a 
need for stability in the budget and a focus on student mental health, while staff highlighted that years of fiscal 
constraints have impacted trust. 
 
These findings became the compass for our work this year. They confirmed that our mission must be to move 
our "One Community" spirit from a theme to a daily practice: welcoming all, lifting each other up, learning with 
purpose, and leading with heart. 
 
Guided by what I learned, we established four strategic goals to steer the district through the 2025- 2026 school 
year. 
 
First, we focused on Improving Instruction. With the appointment of Dr. Kimberly Sarfde as our Director of 
Teaching and Learning, we raised the bar for classroom excellence. Our goal has been to ensure every educator 
receives high-quality, actionable feedback that drives student achievement. A central pillar of this work is our 
steadfast commitment to early literacy, with the explicit goal that every child will be reading fluently by the end 
of third grade. We are seeing the "joy of rigor" emerge in every classroom, where high expectations meet high 
engagement. 
 
Second, we prioritized Fiscal Leadership and Transparency. A critical step in this direction was the hiring of 
Christopher McNamara as our Business Manager. Mr. McNamara has made strong gains in implementing 
accounting practices that are clear and publicly communicated, allowing effective insight for town elected 
officials and citizens. Understanding the financial pressures facing the town, we developed a budget process 
defined by "radical clarity." We presented distinct budget scenarios—level-service, town-allocation, and 
restoration which fostered an honest, collaborative dialogue about what we value and what we fund. 
 
Third, we dedicated ourselves to Building a Culture of Belonging. We strove to create an environment where 
kindness is the norm and every student sees themselves as a valued member of the community. 
 
Looking back on the past year, we have achieved milestones. 
 
 We completed universal screenings in mathematics and literacy for grades K–5 to ensure no child slips through 
the cracks. Our educators modeled the instructional excellence we strive for. To support this work, we provided 
targeted professional development in the Open Sci Ed curriculum and the development of literacy skills. 
84
===PAGE 89===
 
We built from a position of fiscal stability. Throughout the year, our budget remained healthy and on track. Our 
mantra was no surprises for the staff, school committee and the community that entrusts us to be stewards of 
limited resources.  Furthermore, in compliance with state requirements, we responsibly reinvested funds into 
our food services program, upgrading critical infrastructure like walk-in freezers to better serve our students. 
 
 The spirit of Lunenburg shone brightly this year. From the always spirited Homecoming parade to an 
undefeated regular season for our Field Hockey team and a Championship title for our Football team, our 
students gave us much to cheer for. Perhaps most inspiring was our Unified Basketball program, which 
continues to be a beacon of inclusion, proving that we win when we play together. Even in our youngest grades, 
we saw this culture taking root, with Primary School students demonstrating unprompted acts of kindness that 
remind us all of the power of empathy. 
 
As we look toward the coming year, my commitment to the town of Lunenburg remains steadfast: to lead 
visibly, communicate plainly, and budget transparently. We acknowledge the challenges ahead, but we face 
them with the strength of a united community. 
 
Thank you for the opportunity to serve this incredible town. 
 
Respectfully submitted, 
 
Dr. Jodi Fortuna, Superintendent

School Facilities Department 
Town Report 2025 
 
Lunenburg Schools Facilities has been working diligently on the up keep of our infrastructure and the 
completion of several Capital Planning Projects throughout the summer of FY 25. 
Capital Program 
 
All of the projects below have been completed: 
 
l)  Primary Elementary School mini split project was completed at a cost of $499,900 in August of 
FY25. The project included the installation of (13)2-ton condensers, (13)2-ton indoor wall units. These 
units will provide cooling in the warmer months and help out with the buildings original heating 
system as well as act as supplemental heat if needed. Lunenburg has worked with Green Communities 
for grants to help pay the cost as well as rebates from Unitil and National Grid. This project was 
awarded to Daikin Applied. 
 
2) THES Fire Alarm Upgrade was completed August of FY25 at a cost of $354,990. This system 
brought the Turkey Hill Elementary School up to code with all new hardware and software. The 
project was awarded to Relay Fire & Safety. 
 
3) Exterior door upgrade at Turkey Hill Elementary was completed August of FY25 at a cost of 
$164,482. This project included the replacement of doors and hardware on entrances 1, 2, 3 and 10. 
This project has helped make the building safer, reliable and weather tight. This project was awarded 
to Suburban Glass and Mirror. 
 
4) Primary and Turkey Hill got an upgrade of cameras and security card access that was 
completed August of FY25 at a cost of $135,426. The project was rewarded to Galaxy Integrated 
Technologies. 
 
5)  All 3 schools received upgraded refrigeration controls of the walk in freezers and refrigerators at 
a cost of $12,778 .This was funded through Green Communities Grants and Utility rebates. These new 
controls help them work more efficiently and cost effective. Grouped in with this is was weather 
stripping for exterior doors at Primary & Elementary.
 
Summer FY25 Facilities Projects 
 
l)  The Lunenburg Schools Facilities Dept. was tasked with moving Central Office. This move was 
from the deteriorating Passios School to Turkey Hill Elementary School. The District hired a moving 
company Esquire Movers at a cost of $9,820. The Maintenance Department continued moving 
anything residual. A storage container was needed and purchased for a cost of $3,575.
 
 
2)  Facilities Department worked with Kitchen Services to make several upgrades in the District 
Schools kitchens. New mechanicals were installed for the Turkey Hill freezer and refrigerator. A new 
dish machine was installed at Primary as well as a new grease trap. New steamer was installed at 
86
===PAGE 91===
LMHS as well as new milk coolers. The projects were funded through Kitchen Services. 
Preventative Maintenance 
 
The Facilities and Grounds Department has been working conscientiously on up keep of grounds and 
snow removal as well as maintaining climate equipment within the schools. Such tasks include 
cleaning of uninvents, replacement of motors, filters, actuators and heating coils. Custodial is always 
taking advantage of vacations and breaks for deep cleanings of all schools. The Facilities Department 
always strives to cut costs and keep work in house as much as possible. This summer was very active 
and as a department we met the challenge. 
 
Respectfully Submitted, 
Christopher Stevens, Director of Facilities

Town Report 2025 
 
The Lunenburg P
ublic Schools Information Technology (IT) Department played a critical role in addressing 
the technology needs both inside and outside of the classroom. The Department also focused heavily on 
building security upgrades. The following projects were completed as part of an emphasis on building 
security: 
1. Turkey Hill Elementary School received additional cameras to cover many blind spots both in and 
out of the building. 
2. Turkey Hill Elementary School also received a new access control system with swipe pads at 
various entrances around the building. 
3. Turkey Hill Elementary School also was given a brand new alarm system as part of the security 
upgrade. 
4. Lunenburg Primary School received additional cameras to cover blind spots as well as had a 
problematic door fixed. 
5. Pending in 2026 is replacement of the door entry cameras at both locations as well as district-wide 
security center replacement to replace end of life equipment. 
 
Solutions supporting cyber safety were also addressed this year. CrowdStrike was added to all windows 
devices to monitor any malicious activities on the staff devices and lab computers. 
GoGuardian was expanded to not only cover the LMHS one-to-one devices but all student 
Chromebooks. Staff Chromebooks were also introduced which are natively more secure. 
 
Technology continued to be a prevalent tool in the classroom. In response, the district continued to support 
devices for each student by providing new devices to 6th and 9th graders as well as introducing new devices in 
grades 2 and 3 to replace end of life equipment. 
 
The IT Department continued to manage and respond to all help desk requests with an extra focus on 
following best practices, student technology usage and excellent customer service. The team will continue 
to direct efforts towards improving end-user experience and protecting the district’s technology 
environment from potential cyber threats in the new year.

From the March 1, 2025 report (SIMS Report 5-Enrollment Statistics – District Summary), there are 246 
students with disabilities receiving special education or related services in Lunenburg (last year there were 242).  
This represents 15.7% of our total student population of 1,568 students (it was 15.5% last year).  The state 
average from the 2024- 2025 elected Populations Report (data was last updated on January 23, 2025) 
(https://profiles.doe.mass.edu/statereport/selectedpopulations.aspx) is 20.6%.  Fr om the March 1, 2025 report 
(SIMS Report 7 -SPED Enrollment Statistics – District Summary), the total number of students receiving 
services outside the district was 10 (last year was 8).  Of the 10 students, 6 are placed in Collaborative 
Placements, 3 are in Day Placements and 1 is in a Residential Placement.  Our Advanced Community 
Experience Program (ACE), which serves students ages 18- 22 years old, had 10 students enrolled last year.  
Additionally, from the March 1, 2025 report, 122 students identified with a disability received accommodations 
via 504 plans in Lunenburg (last year was 119). 
For the 2025 school year, our Accountability Overall Classification was “not requiring assistance or 
intervention”.  The reason for this classification was “Substantial Progress Towards Targets” at 50% (last year it 
was 60%).  This data can be found at the DESE website:

Teaching & Learning Update 
Town Report 2025 
Teaching and learning remains the central focus of Lunenburg Public Schools. During the 2024–2025 school 
year, the district continued to strengthen curriculum implementation, instructional practices, and systems of 
student support across all grade levels. This work reflects a sustained commitment to research-based instruction, 
data-informed decision-making, and long-term instructional coherence, carried out amid ongoing fiscal 
constraints and increasing uncertainty in state and federal funding. 
Collectively, these instructional systems are designed to ensure that students receive consistent, high-quality 
90
===PAGE 95===
instruction every day, regardless of school or grade level. The district’s emphasis on strong core instruction, 
early identification of need, and timely, targeted support is intended to prevent small gaps from becoming long-
term barriers to learning. For students and families, this work results in clearer expectations, earlier 
intervention, and improved access to grade-level learning. 
Across content areas, the district has prioritized instructional approaches that emphasize conceptual 
understanding, reasoning, and application while maintaining alignment with the Massachusetts Curriculum 
Frameworks and expectations established by the Massachusetts Department of Elementary and Secondary 
Education. 
Literacy Instruction and Student Support Systems 
Lunenburg Public Schools continues to implement a coherent, research-aligned literacy system designed to 
improve outcomes for all students while maximizing the impact of limited and increasingly uncertain resources. 
Rather than relying on isolated or short-term initiatives, the district has focused on aligning curriculum, 
assessment, professional learning, and intervention into an integrated system that can be sustained over time. 
High-quality Tier 1 classroom instruction serves as the foundation of the district’s literacy framework. Research 
consistently demonstrates that strong daily instruction is the most effective way to improve literacy outcomes at 
scale, particularly in districts with limited supplemental staffing. At the elementary level (Grades K–5), HMH 
Into Reading provides a structured, evidence-based approach that integrates foundational skills, vocabulary 
development, knowledge-building, and comprehension. 
In Grades K–2, instruction emphasizes early reading development—including phonological awareness, phonics, 
spelling, and fluency—while intentionally embedding oral language and comprehension from the outset. These 
practices are designed to prevent reading difficulty rather than respond after gaps widen. In Grades 3–5, 
instruction shifts toward consolidating fluent reading and strengthening vocabulary, background knowledge, 
and comprehension of increasingly complex texts. 
At the middle school level, the district has devoted significant time to deepening implementation of Amplify 
English Language Arts. Educators engage in sustained Professional Learning Community (PLC) work focused 
on text complexity, pacing, instructional routines, and scaffolding to ensure students consistently engage with 
rigorous, grade-level material while receiving appropriate instructional support. 
Assessment data are used instructionally to guide teaching and support decisions. Elementary students are 
universally screened using Dynamic Indicators of Basic Literacy Skills (DIBELS), enabling educators to 
identify risk early, adjust instruction, and provide targeted intervention through the district’s Multi-Tiered 
System of Supports (MTSS). Intervention time and intensity are determined by data and refined through regular 
progress monitoring and collaborative review. 
Beginning in February 2026, Lunenburg Primary School will engage in a short, high-impact partnership with 
the HILL for Literacy. This classroom-based work will focus on modeling effective instruction and providing 
real-time feedback to teachers, intentionally strengthening internal instructional capacity that can be sustained 
despite declining external funding. 
Mathematics Instruction and Conceptual Understanding 
Mathematics instruction across grades K–12 emphasizes conceptual understanding, reasoning, and application 
rather than rote memorization of procedures. Programs and practices are aligned with the Massachusetts 
Mathematics Frameworks and DESE expectations for high-quality instruction. 
In Grades K–5, mathematics instruction is delivered through HMH Into Math, with a strong emphasis on 
number sense, multiple representations, mathematical discourse, and problem-solving. Students regularly 
engage with visual models, hands-on materials, and structured discussion to develop deep and lasting 
91
===PAGE 96===
understanding of mathematical concepts. 
At the middle school level, mathematics instruction is delivered through EdGems, an inquiry-based program 
that emphasizes reasoning, sense-making, and productive struggle. Instruction encourages students to articulate 
their thinking, engage with complex problems, and develop confidence as mathematical learners. 
At the high school level, mathematics instruction follows a coherent sequence aligned with state frameworks 
and college- and career-readiness expectations. Students complete required coursework in Algebra, Geometry, 
and Algebra II, with additional opportunities in Precalculus, Statistics, Calculus, Business Math, and Advanced 
Placement courses. Multiple pathways ensure access to rigorous, standards-aligned instruction that supports a 
range of postsecondary goals. 
Universal screening for early numeracy using the Universal Screener for Number Sense (USNS) supports 
MTSS decision-making in the elementary grades. Data are reviewed collaboratively to guide instruction, 
intervention, and progress monitoring. 
Science Instruction and Inquiry-Based Learning 
Science instruction across grades K–12 is aligned with the Massachusetts Science and Technology/Engineering 
Frameworks and the Next Generation Science Standards. Instruction emphasizes inquiry, investigation, and the 
application of scientific concepts. 
In Grades K–2, science instruction is delivered through locally designed, standards-aligned units that promote 
curiosity, observation, questioning, and hands-on exploration, supporting early scientific thinking and language 
development. 
In Grades 3–5, science instruction utilizes OpenSciEd materials, which engage students in real-world 
phenomena and integrate science and engineering practices, disciplinary core ideas, and crosscutting concepts. 
This approach supports sustained investigation, explanation, and sense-making over time. 
Middle school science is delivered through Glencoe/McGraw Hill iScience, incorporating hands-on 
investigations, laboratory experiences, literacy connections, and assessments aligned with state standards. 
At the high school level, students complete coursework in Biology and have additional opportunities in 
Chemistry, Physics, Environmental Science, Anatomy and Physiology, Biotechnology, and Advanced 
Placement sciences. Instruction emphasizes laboratory experiences, scientific inquiry, and the application of 
scientific concepts. 
Data-Informed Instruction, MTSS, and Professional Collaboration 
Across content areas, structured data review and professional collaboration remain central to instructional 
improvement. Professional Learning Communities meet regularly to analyze student performance, refine 
instructional practices, and adjust MTSS supports. Progress is reviewed through ongoing data analysis, 
classroom observation, and collaborative reflection to ensure instructional practices remain effective, equitable, 
and responsive to student need. 
This work reflects the expertise, professionalism, and sustained commitment of Lunenburg educators, who 
continue to refine practice and support students despite significant resource constraints. 
Safety, Preparedness, and School Environment 
Student and staff safety remains a district priority. Staff have participated in ALICE safety training, and schools 
maintain strong partnerships with local police and fire departments to support emergency preparedness and age-
appropriate safety education. 
 
92
===PAGE 97===
Federal and State Funding: Strategic Use and Uncertainty 
The district continues to use federal ESSA Title funds strategically to support instructional priorities, 
professional learning, intervention services, and capacity-building aligned with identified student needs. 
However, the future of these funding sources is increasingly uncertain. 
Lunenburg Public Schools does not anticipate receiving its full allocation of Title I funds in future years and is 
actively planning for potential reductions. In addition, the district is preparing for the likely loss of Title II and 
Title IV funding moving forward. These funds have historically supported professional development, 
instructional leadership, and student support initiatives that cannot be readily absorbed into the local operating 
budget. 
As funding uncertainty increases, the district has prioritized protecting the instructional core. This includes 
maintaining high-quality curriculum, preserving early literacy and numeracy screening, sustaining MTSS 
structures, and ensuring educators have access to the professional collaboration necessary to respond effectively 
to student needs. 
The district is also actively pursuing additional grant opportunities to offset anticipated losses and support 
continued instructional improvement. Despite these efforts, the loss of federal funding will further limit 
flexibility and place additional pressure on already constrained instructional systems. 
Fiscal Context and Sustainability 
All instructional work described in this report has been carried out amid repeated budget reductions, staffing 
losses, declining grant funding, and growing uncertainty surrounding federal education dollars. These fiscal 
realities have required careful prioritization and an ongoing focus on instructional coherence and sustainability. 
 
The district is operating with limited remaining flexibility. Continued progress depends on stability in staffing 
and funding. Additional reductions—particularly the loss of federal Title funds—would compromise 
instructional systems and place current gains at risk. 
 
Looking Ahead 
Lunenburg Public Schools remains committed to continuous improvement, transparency, and collaboration with 
families and the community. The district’s instructional systems are designed to ensure that access to high 
quality instruction and timely support is based on student need rather than circumstance. 
By investing in coherent curriculum, strong instruction, and data-informed systems of support, the district 
continues to work toward improved outcomes for all students while responsibly managing limited and uncertain 
resources. 
Respectfully Submitted, 
Dr. Kimberly Sarfde, Director of Teaching & Learning

As the Commonwealth of Massachusetts continues to supplement the National School Lunch Program, all 
students are able to access healthy school meals regardless of household income. Universal free meals have 
eliminated the stigma often associated with qualifying for free and reduced-price meals, and student 
participation continues to grow as a result. 
Increased meal participation and reimbursement revenue have allowed the program to make several much-
needed equipment upgrades across district kitchens. At the Primary School, A new dish machine was installed. 
Turkey Hill Elementary School upgraded the walk-in freezer and purchased a new slicer. The Middle High 
School replaced the steamer and milk coolers. 
LINQ Nutrition Software has proven to be a valuable asset to the program, supporting menu planning and 
nutrition analysis while saving staff time. Additionally, allowing students to enter their own identification 
numbers during meal service has significantly improved efficiency. 
The Lunch Program continues to participate in Harvest of the month promoting a different Massachusetts-
produced food each month in our cafeterias. This initiative supports local farms and encourages students to 
enjoy locally grown foods. 
We also welcomed a new employee to our team, Katelyn Walsh.
