Lunenburg Primary School 
Town Report 2025 
Lunenburg Primary School is a student-centered early childhood school serving children from preschool 
through Grade 2. Educating students ages three through eight, we remain committed to fostering a nurturing, 
inclusive, and engaging environment where each child’s academic, social, and emotional development is 
supported. Our school community consists of students, families, educators, instructional assistants, and support 
staff working collaboratively to ensure that every child’s individual needs are met. By focusing on the whole 
child, we strive to cultivate strong foundational academic skills alongside the essential social-emotional 
competencies that prepare students for continued success throughout their educational journey. We are proud to 
maintain a welcoming and enriching atmosphere where all children feel valued and encouraged to reach their 
full potential, and we look forward to another year of shared growth, learning, and community. 
As of February 2026, Lunenburg Primary School reported a total enrollment of 406 students to the Department 
of Elementary and Secondary Education. This includes 65 preschool students attending various half-day 
94
===PAGE 99===
sessions across two classrooms. The school serves 110 Kindergarten students across five classrooms, with class 
sizes ranging from 21 to 23 students; 119 Grade 1 students across five classrooms, with class sizes of 23 to 24 
students; and 112 Grade 2 students across five classrooms, with class sizes ranging from 22 to 24 students. This 
structure ensures that each grade level is supported by dedicated classroom spaces designed to promote optimal 
learning conditions. We remain proud to provide a strong academic foundation that supports both student 
achievement and developmental growth. 
Throughout the 2025–2026 school year, our professional development efforts have centered on strengthening 
inclusive practices and refining high-quality instructional strategies. We continue to implement the HMH Into 
Reading and Into Math curricula to guide literacy and mathematics instruction. Common Planning Time 
remains a priority within our schedule, enabling teachers to collaborate regularly, analyze student performance 
data, and align instruction to meet the needs of all learners. In partnership with consultants and district 
curriculum coordinators, our staff continues to deepen the use of evidence-based practices and ensure a 
consistent and cohesive learning experience across grade levels. 
In addition to academic excellence, we place strong emphasis on cultivating a positive, inclusive, and 
supportive school culture. Our educators consistently work to create classroom environments where students 
feel safe, respected, and empowered. The Responsive Classroom model continues to be implemented school 
wide to strengthen community and promote social-emotional learning. We also maintain our commitment to 
Positive Behavioral Interventions and Supports (PBIS), a tiered framework designed to enhance student 
behavior and engagement through aligned systems and practices. Monthly school wide themes encourage 
reflection and character development among students and staff. Through our continued partnership with our 
School Resource Officer and his emotional support canine, Hank, we celebrate students through the Hank’s 
Helper Awards, recognizing acts of kindness, respect, and helpfulness within our school community. During 
monthly assemblies, selected students also participate in “Snack with the Principal,” providing opportunities for 
small-group connection and student voice. These initiatives reflect our dedication to supporting both academic 
achievement and character development. 
Lunenburg Primary School remains committed to ensuring that all students receive the support necessary to 
succeed. We continue refining our tiered instructional system to provide timely and targeted interventions. 
Students in Kindergarten through Grade 2 participate in common reading and mathematics assessments in the 
fall, winter, and spring to monitor progress and evaluate instructional effectiveness. Using this data, we identify 
students who may require additional support and provide targeted interventions through our Multi-Tiered 
System of Supports (MTSS). This year, we were pleased to secure two no-cost reading intervention programs 
for the district:  High Dosage Tutoring and Hill for Literacy to further strengthen our literacy support services. 
Our MTSS framework provides individualized interventions in reading, mathematics, speech and language, 
occupational therapy, and social-emotional support. Through a comprehensive and data-driven approach, we 
aim to ensure that every student is equipped with the tools and resources needed to thrive. 
The Lunenburg Primary School Council continues to meet monthly to review school initiatives, advise the 
principal, and contribute to the development of the school improvement plan and building budget. The 2025–
2026 Council members include Principal Carla Squier; Assistant Principal Michael Smith; parents Skylar Joly-
Pelliter, Janet Winer, Sarah Wright, and Ryan Hilgendorf; School Committee Representative Mandy Gilman; 
and School Building Leadership Team members Jennifer Hochard, Amy Raffaele, Christa Palma, Julie Hudson, 
and Laura Refrigeri. The Council’s collaborative efforts and diverse perspectives ensure that decisions are made 
in the best interest of our students and school community. 
We are sincerely grateful for the opportunity to work alongside our dedicated district colleagues, supportive 
families, and engaged community partners in serving Lunenburg’s youngest learners. The continued 
collaboration and commitment of our school community make a meaningful difference in the lives of our 
students each day. For more information about Lunenburg Primary School, please visit the Lunenburg Primary 
School website.

Carla Squier, Principal

Turkey Hill Elementary School 
Town Report 2025 
 
Turkey Hill Elementary School is an elementary school serving students in third grade through fifth grade.  As 
of Feb 2026, Turkey Hill Elementary School serves 355 enrolled students. There are five sections of grade 3, 
grade 4, and grade 5.  Grade 3 has 111 students. Grade 4 has 136 students and Grade 5 has 108 students. 
Our unified arts areas include physical education, art, music, library, and STEM. We are also served by a full 
time assistant principal, full time nurse, a psychologist, a full time adjustment counselor/social worker, and 21 
full time teachers.  Other support personnel who work across the district include an ELA coach (through 
December), a Math Specialist, a Director of Teaching and Learning, two EL teachers, a Speech and Language 
Pathologist, OT, and PT.  In addition, we have a half-time interventionist targeted support for small groups of 
students.  
Staff continually work on the development of the whole child by supporting students' development and growth 
in the areas of social emotional learning, literacy, math, social studies and science.  We are able to meet student 
needs with the support of the district curriculum of teaching and learning and the two coaches which enables 
teachers to have the opportunity to meet at grade levels to develop and provide evidence based instruction, 
common learning experiences, and student data analysis and share effective teaching strategies to support 
student academic growth.  Students continue to have benchmark assessments three times throughout the school 
year (beginning, middle and end of year) that are used to help us celebrate students’ academic growth and 
effort.   The data provided by these assessments also helps refine our focus for interventions.  We began the 
implementation of the OpenSci Ed curriculum with initial training and a trial unit.  The implementation will 
continue next school year.  
This fall our fifth grade students had their annual trip to Nature's Classroom. It was a successful week with good 
weather
, community building, and the creation of fond memories of a unique time together, thanks to the 
combined efforts of our entire fifth grade staff. 
We held an open house in September. It was a well-attended event and for many parents their first opportunity 
to be physically present in our building.  It was accompanied by a book fair facilitated by our librarian, Mrs. 
McNamara. Parent conferences in January offered both in person and virtual options and appointments were 
fully booked within a few days.  
We held our annual Penny Wars fundraiser, it was a huge success and the money raised will be put toward the 
purchase and installation of a shade structure in our playing field.  As always, our PTO assisted in the weeklong 
collection. We could not be successful without their efforts. We were able to bring back a fifth grade band and 
all students will be performing in an upcoming spring concert under the direction of our music teacher, Mr. 
Anthony Balester.  Turkey Hill also held its annual Turkey Trot in November.  Led by Mr. Derek Guimond, the 
Turkey Trot was a wonderful experience.

===PAGE 102===
The Turkey Hill School Council meets each month to discuss school related concerns.  Serving as an advisory 
group to the principal, the council is charged with reviewing the school building budget and developing the 
school improvement plan.

Lunenburg Middle School 
Town Report 2025 
 
Lunenburg Middl
e School provides a rigorous academic curriculum as well as strong programs that support 
students’ social and emotional growth. These programs ensure that each child becomes a self-motivated, 
creative, lifelong learner in a safe, stimulating environment that encourages diversity and open honest 
communication. The dedicated and accomplished staff of professionals within the school embraces the 
philosophy of educating the whole child academically, socially, and emotionally. This philosophy is a means by 
which all students become positive community members that possess the skills to be successful in high school 
and beyond. 
 Lunenburg Middle School is a co-located middle - high school serving grades six through eight. Our effective, 
student centered middle school embraces interdisciplinary teams of teachers that enable teachers to work with 
smaller groups of students. Smaller groups of students allow teachers to learn and engage students in their 
preferred learning style, making them more confident and successful. 
Our effective middle school model is student centered and provides opportunities to explore a variety of 
different interests and talents through special areas and enrichment classes and extracurricular activities. One of 
our goals is for students to discover something they are passionate about and want to continue exploring as they 
move through high school and beyond. We will continue to seek out opportunities for students that foster 
critical thinking and problem-solving skills that prepare them for high school and beyond or where their passion 
may lead them to. 
Our grade eight team of teachers continue to implement a schedule that mirrors the high school schedule. Our 
philosophy is to ease the difficult transition from middle school to the first year of high school by developing 
student’s independent skills they will require to be successful in high school. Grade eight teachers function as 
99
===PAGE 104===
one inclusive team where students take classes with a variety of different teachers as they would in a high 
school setting. This differs from our two interdisciplinary team of teachers that have been a successful model in 
grades six and seven.    
 Lunenburg Middle School has benefited from a top notch guidance support team. A guidance counselor and a 
school Adjustment Counselor coupled with our shared school psychologist has been able to provide a high level 
of social emotional support to our students and their families. Our guidance team has been able to meet the 
needs of our students while preparing them to be available for academic learning.

compared to other schools in the state that service similar grade levels.

Lunenburg Middle School continues to provide high quality educational services and supports that ensure 
student success and promote a passion for lifelong learning. The key components to our success continue to be 
our talented faculty committed to making a positive impact on students, our parents and community who 
support our teaching and learning, and most of all, our students who inspire us through their commitment to 
learning. Together we continue to make a difference in the lives of students.

Lunenburg High School 
Town Report 2025 
 
 
Lunenburg High School and its faculty continue to focus on rigorous curriculum, high standards and 
opportunities for students, both academic and extracurricular, so that every student feels supported and included 
in our community. LHS faculty is student centered in their teaching practices, and strive to learn and to change 
in order to best serve all of our students.  
 
2025 School Year 
 
Lunenburg High School is rich with tradition and core values that center around student success. A talented and 
experienced faculty are skilled in supporting students in their learning and creating an environment that fosters 
independence and resilience. Our goal is for students is to acquire the skills necessary to earn a high school 
diploma and be successful in post-secondary education as well as in the work force. 
  
A reduction in faculty and opportunities for student choice have challenged our school community to adapt and 
shift to best meet the ever changing needs of the students we serve. We continue with an upward trend in both 
the number of students taking Advanced Placement coursework as well as the percentage of students scoring a 3 
or higher in those courses.  
 
We have also developed programs that support students in their daily classroom instruction. We are fortunate to 
have an Intervention teacher that provides direct instruction to students who were identified by their teachers as 
to needing additional support to be successful in the classroom. Students receive instruction in the specific class 
or classes they are struggling in. The data show the students participating in the intervention classroom show 
substantial improvement in the completion of classwork and in their overall grades. 
 
MCAS RESULTS 
Lunenburg High School - Spring 2025 MCAS Results (Grade 10) 
 
Subject Test Exceeding Expectations / 
Meeting Expectations 
State Average (EE/ME) 
ELA 58% 51%

The results indicate the LHS percentage scores for meeting and exceeding expectations are at or higher than the 
state average in all tested areas. In mathematics, 8 percentage points higher than the state average when 
compared to schools who serve students in similar grade levels.  
 
We were also very pleased to learn that 29 students at Lunenburg High School received the state’s John and 
Abigail Adams Scholarship this year.  The scholarship is available to students whose MCAS performance 
places them at the top 25 percent of their district.  To be eligible, students must either score Advanced on one 
exam and Proficient on the other, or Advanced on both the English and Math exams.  That is approximately 30 
% of grade 10 students receiving the award.   
 
HOMECOMING, ATHLETICS AND EXTRACURRICULAR ACTIVITIES 
 
Homecoming weekend was a wonderful and well attended by students, families and alumni. The weather was 
beautiful and highlighted the community event. The parade was a huge success with a huge turnout to support 
103
===PAGE 108===
the school. Many community members noticed the care and time that were placed into the floats at each grade 
level.  The Blue Knights played well and won the football game and we always enjoy watching the LHMS 
Marching Band perform.    
 
Lunenburg High School Athletic Accomplishments 
Fall 
The golf team delivered an outstanding season, finishing 9-1 in league play and 10-1 overall in the postseason, 
earning the title of Mid Wach C League Champions. To cap off an exceptional year, one of our student-athletes 
was honored as the league MVP. 
 
The girls’ soccer team also enjoyed a remarkable season, posting an impressive league record of 8-1-3 and an 
overall postseason record of 13-2-3. Their consistent excellence earned them the title of Mid Wach C League 
Champions. 
 
The boys’ soccer team demonstrated tremendous resilience and perseverance throughout the season. While the 
record did not fully reflect their effort and growth, the team’s commitment to integrity and teamwork was 
recognized with the prestigious Sportsmanship Award for their league. 
The field hockey team finished in dominant fashion with an undefeated league record of 6-0-3, securing the Mid 
Wach C League Championship. They concluded the postseason with an outstanding 12-1-4 overall record and 
were further recognized by Central Mass officials for exemplary sportsmanship. 
 
The cross country program had another strong year. The girls’ team was recognized for outstanding 
sportsmanship in their league, while the boys’ team showed grit and determination through the final meets of 
the season. Two of our student-athletes were honored as Telegram & Gazette All-Stars, highlighting the 
program’s continued success. 
 
Football captured the Mid Wach E League Championship with an impressive 7-3 record. The team’s success 
was further highlighted by two student-athletes earning league MVP nominations. 
 
Cheerleading continued to build momentum this fall, earning placements at several competitions while also 
seeing encouraging growth in participation numbers. 
 
Unified basketball had an incredible season, culminating at the Fitchburg High School Jamboree. The event 
showcased the true spirit of unified athletics, as it was inspiring to see all of our student-athletes competing and 
celebrating together. 
 
At the middle school level, both cross country and field hockey enjoyed exciting seasons focused on skill 
development and growth. Coaches reported noticeable improvement and strong enthusiasm from our 
developing student-athletes. 
 
Winter 
The indoor track team has put together a tremendous season competing at both the Reggie Lewis Center and 
New Balance in Boston—elite venues that serve as the only sites where performances are officially recognized 
for meeting MIAA qualifying standards. The girls team achieved the remarkable distinction of being crowned 
Division 5 State Champions, while the boys team delivered an outstanding performance, finishing just behind 
them and placing 6th place out of 40 schools. Many hitting personal records and medaling at the meet. 
Boys basketball, currently holding an 11-7 record, has advanced to the finals of the Clark Tournament—one of 
the oldest and most respected tournaments in the country. This marks the program’s first finals appearance in 34 
years, with the team set to compete on February 20th. They have also secured a postseason berth as the #25 seed 
in Division 4. 
 
Girls basketball has also had a strong campaign, posting a 12-7 record and earning a Division 4 postseason 
104
===PAGE 109===
berth as the #20 seed. While they fell short in the Clark Tournament, the team’s consistent play has positioned 
them well for tournament competition. 
 
Winter cheer made an exciting return this year for the first time in several years, and participation numbers were 
strong. The team has played a key role in reigniting school spirit and bringing great energy and hype to boys 
basketball games. They have also attended several competitions this season and are eager to earn placements as 
the program continues to grow. 
 
The ski team, a cooperative program with Littleton, Narragansett, and Leominster, has competed well at 
multiple meets this season, with several student-athletes advancing to their respective tournaments. 
 
The ice hockey team, currently 12-6-1, has put together an impressive season and earned the #16 seed heading 
into postseason play. 
 
Our cooperative girls hockey team at Leominster High School includes three dedicated student-athletes from 
Lunenburg. The team is currently seeded #22 in Division 2 and will continue into postseason competition. 
 
Our middle school basketball teams recently concluded one of their most engaging seasons to date. With many 
new participants and clear skill development on display at home contests, the future of Blue Knights basketball 
looks extremely promising as these young student-athletes continue to grow and refine their game. 
CURRICULUM 
Teachers and administrators continue to refine, revise and create curriculum that is aligned to the state 
frameworks and is rigorous and challenging. This year’s focus is to teachers will be to engage in NEASC -
aligned curriculum work and unit planning to support coherent, standards -based instruction across content 
areas. Professional development time will be used to review, refine, and align instructional units to ensure 
consistency, intentional sequencing, and alignment with school wide expectations for teaching and learning.  
 
Lunenburg High School continues to provide a high quality education that ensures student success and 
promotes a passion for lifelong learning.  The key components to our success continue to be our talented 
faculty, our parents and our community members who support our teaching and learning. Most of all, our 
students who inspire us through their commitment to learning. Together we continue to make the difference in 
the lives of students.

As the Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School District approaches its 
upcoming 60th anniversary, it is apparent that our school community refuses to rest on its 
past laurels and successes. Instead, Monty Tech continues to seek out and implement 
innovative strategies and partnerships that will best address our diverse student body’s 
post-secondary goals as they proceed on their workforce or educational pathways. 
Personally, it marked my eleventh year at Monty Tech and third as its very fortunate 
superintendent as I continue to be so appreciative of the commitment to success of our 
students, families, instructors, staff, administrators and community partners. As always, I 
am honored to provide our eighteen cities and towns with this annual report which 
summarizes the District’s achievements and innovations during the 2024-2025 school 
year. 
 
As the school year opened, Monty Tech experienced a welcome growth spurt as the District officially launched the 
inaugural year of the Montachusett Vocational Partnership (MVP) Academy. Created as an innovative proposal to 
address the building capacity issue that results in hundreds of students being deprived of the opportunity to be 
enrolled at Monty Tech, MVP Academy allowed nearly 80 juniors and seniors from three of our sending districts, 
the opportunity to receive workforce training in Electrical or House Carpentry during the school day while remaining 
enrolled in their sending school. Thus, as Monty Tech instructors and students worked to complete the renovation of 
the program’s future home, the initial MVP 
instruction began in an outdoor classroom 
located          on our main campus. As winter 
approached, however, the MVP students joined 
their Monty Tech peers to assist with the facility 
renovations and, in April 2025, Monty Tech 
welcomed dignitaries from throughout the 
commonwealth to simultaneously celebrate the 
grand opening of the MVP Academy and the 
graduation ceremony of the program’s first 40 
graduates. Looking ahead, the program hopes to 
expand by adding a third program, Plumbing, in the 
fall of 2026. 
 
Of course, innovative programs like MVP Academy are not possible without the District’s efforts to alleviate the 
burden of costly new initiatives that could fall on the eighteen cities and towns of our regional community. 
Consequently, in order to avoid that scenario, 2024-2025 became noteworthy as the District’s most successful in 
terms of accessing competitive grants from a variety of resources. This included opportunities involving 
cybersecurity, equitable access, workforce training in clean energy fields and for adults with disabilities, skills 
capital equipment, partnership/implementation programs, no-cost adult workforce training and even anonymous 
corporate partner donations. In total, during the 2024-2025 school year, Monty Tech was awarded almost $3.7 
million in external grants and donations. Such results clearly reflect the District’s commitment to offering innovative 
state-of-the-art workforce training to a variety of highly skilled workers while also being mindful of the financial 
implications such valuable training could have on our sending communities.  
 
Without question, the MVP program represents a significant expansion of workforce training that goes beyond our 
annual secondary-level training efforts. However, it is important to acknowledge the ongoing efforts of our post -
secondary training programs. For example, our Continuing Education program, also known as Monty Tech Nights, 
maintained its consecutive run of expanding enrollment across the spectrum of its offerings. Between the 2020-2021 
and the 2024-2025 school years, Monty Tech Nights has experienced 25-30% growth in its enrollment. An essential 
element of this growth has been the expanded offerings of our no- cost training through the Career Technical 
108
===PAGE 113===
Initiative (CTI). In 2024- 2025 alone, the efforts in CTI resulted in 108 graduates in HVAC, Plumbing, Auto Body 
and Carpentry. Furthermore, the District, once again, applied for the most recent round of grants through CTI and 
was awarded the Round 10 CTI grant. Meanwhile, in June, the District’s Practical Nursing Program held its 
ceremonial “pinning” of 45 graduates who completed their training during the 2024-2025 school year. Monty Tech 
takes great pride in the fact that so many of these well-trained nurses choose to work within our local communities as 
nearly half of the graduates reported that they would be working for local healthcare providers including Gardner 
Rehabilitation, The Highlands, Fitchburg Healthcare, Athol Hospital, Dept. of Developmental Services, CAPS 
Collaborative, Heyward Affiliated Medical Group, MCI Gardner, Wachusett Manor and Spring Hill Recovery 
Center. 
 
Additionally, while expansive programs like MVP Academy, Monty Tech Nights and the Practical Nursing Program 
are certainly elements of our district that we take great pride in supporting, we have the same sense of pride when it 
comes to the small individual equipment purchases or facility renovations that happen on a year- to-year basis. For 
example, during the 2024-2025 school year, through a variety of fiscal resources including annual Chapter 74 funds 
from state aid, Monty Tech introduced new appliances to our culinary program, supplemented the HVAC program’s 
energy efficiency equipment, installed new rooftop air conditioning equipment, replaced flooring in our student 
support classrooms and repaired the roof to our auditorium entry way. Such ongoing efforts to maintain a clean and 
safe learning environment, as well as a well -equipped instructional tool kit, are the backbone to ensuring that our 
students earn the industry recognized credentials and state/national SkillsUSA awards that indicate our vocational 
excellence. It should not be overlooked that, in 2024-2025, thanks to the wonderful support of our industry partners 
across the region, Monty Tech had a record- breaking year as 288 students were placed at cooperative learning sites 
allowing them to have real world industry training while earning money as an employee.  
 
Similarly, it is these critical efforts that allow for our consistent academic achievement as demonstrated by our 
MCAS Passing Rates (97% in ELA, 95% in Math and 95% in Biology) and our growing Advanced Placement 
results including 2024-2025 as Monty Tech experienced its highest number of AP exams administered (292), highest 
number of students earning a qualifying score (135) and, notably, nearly doubling the number of students who were 
designated AP Scholars for earning a qualifying score on three different AP exams (35). 
 
To conclude, while it is clear that there will be changes to traditional vocational school operations across the 
Commonwealth, Monty Tech will continue to focus on overall student achievement, outstanding instructional 
practice and the support for our sending communities. Without question, as an institution, we take great pride in the 
numerous achievements highlighted in this annual report, but we additionally acknowledge that our success as a 
school is dependent on the ongoing support of the civic leadership and residents of our eighteen communities. 
 
On behalf of everyone at Monty Tech, we are grateful for that continued support and greatly value the opportunity to 
be an educational partner to all of our communities.    
 
Respectfully submitted, 
 
Thomas R. Browne, Superintendent-Director

 Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School—known throughout the region as Monty Tech—
is a four-year career and technical high school serving eighteen member communities: 
 
 
Ashburnham 
Ashby 
Athol 
Barre 
Fitchburg 
Gardner 
Harvard 
Holden 
Hubbardston 
Lunenburg 
Petersham 
Phillipston 
Princeton 
Royalston 
Sterling 
Templeton 
Westminster 
Winchendon 
 
 
 
 
 
During the 2024-2025 school year, Monty Tech served 1,417 
students in grades 9 –12 across 21 rigorous vocational -
technical programs, with a balanced enrollment of male and 
female students representing a wide range of academic 
interests and achievements. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
110
===PAGE 115===
Each year, Monty Tech’s Business Manager works in conjunction with the Superintendent and 22- member 
School Committee, to develop a cost -effective budget that reflects the values and priorities of the institution.  
The final fiscal year 2024- 2025 Educational Plan totaled $33,334,174 which represented a modest 2.46% 
increase over the 2023 -2024 Educational Plan.  Remarkably, Monty Tech has been able to deliver highly 
effective, meaningful academic and vocational training programs, operating on one of the Commonwealth’s 
more conservative budgets.  In fact, the FY25 budget exceeded the minimum spending required by 
Massachusetts General Law Chapter 70 by only 0.8%, while other Massachusetts vocational school districts 
operated on budgets that exceeded the FY25 Required Net School Spending by as much as 65%.  Further, a 
historical review of district expenses confirms a commitment to provide resources where they benefit 
students most – instruction.   
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
While we are proud to offer vital education and training programs at a minimal cost to our member 
communities, we understand that vocational education is, in fact, more costly than a traditional, 
comprehensive academic program.  To that end, school officials pursue countless grant opportunities to 
expand and improve training programs each year.   For the fiscal year 2025, state and federal grant sources 
provided the district with $4,728,144.  Programs funded by these grants include: Special Education Services 
and Program Improvement, Title I Support, Perkins Occupational Education, Vocational Capital Equipment 
Enhancements, Student Support and Academic Enrichment, and No-Cost Evening Training Initiatives.

Like almost all vocational schools across 
Massachusetts, Monty Tech has a limited 
building capacity, which drives the school’s 
acceptance.  When more students apply for 
admission than there are available seats, 
Monty Tech has, for many years, relied on 
the state -approved admission policy to 
determine which students would be 
accepted.  Guided by the District 
Agreement, which ensures students from all 
eighteen sending communities are accepted annually, the school’s admission policy has undergone intense 
review.  In 2024- 2025, school leaders determined that the admission policy, which has always included 
scoring elements like grades, attendance, discipline, and even a personal interview, would change moving 
forward.  Beginning 2025-2026, students will be accepted by lottery, removing merit -based elements such as 
grades or discipline.  While we understand that a lottery will not address the overarching issue of capacity, 
we remain committed to increasing access through innovative programming, like MVP Academy and no-
cost training programs through the School of Continuing Education, which (combined ) provide vocational 
training to approximately 200 more students annually.  With a waitlist exceeding 400 students each year, we 
still have work to do and look forward to creative solutions and meaningful partnerships with area 
comprehensive school districts and even employers who can help us address increasing student interest and 
workforce demands across North Central MA.    
 
In our 60- year history, Monty Tech has 
earned a reputation for providing 
innovative, engaging lessons that produce 
students and graduates that feed our 
regional workforce.  While our reputation 
is strong, and our graduates are successful 
contributors, we are often met with the 
same questions – why do so many of your 
students go to college after graduation?  
Shouldn’t they be ready to enter the 
workforce?  At Monty Tech, first and 
foremost, we aim to provide students with 
a well-rounded high school education that 
prepares the student for both college and 
career pathways, because roughly half of our vocational programs require continued education and training 
to enter the labor market, earning a livable wage (i.e. Engineering Technology, Health Occupations, Early 
Childhood Education).  The other half of our vocational training programs do not require a college degree to 
be competitive in the workforce.  Students who graduate from programs such as Carpentry, Masonry, and 
Cosmetology are prepared for entry -level positions within their trades.  Having such a range of workforce 
training programs demands that we prepare students for college and/or career upon graduation.  It is not 
surprising, then, to see that 34% of the Class of 2025 entered the workforce, while another 57% enrolled at a 
2 or 4-year college or university.  What should be noted, though, are those students who do both.  So often 
students who enroll in a 2-year college (24% of the Class of 2025) are balancing their continued studies with 
part-time (or even full- time) employment.  Those students, no doubt, are relying on the skills they gained at 
Monty Tech, to help as they work their way through a degree program. 
112
===PAGE 117===
 
 
 
While students attending Monty Tech benefit from the hands -on, project -based learning that defines a 
vocational-technical high school experience, they are also held to the same rigorous academic expectations 
as their peers in comprehensive districts. At Monty Tech, we believe a well -rounded high school education 
includes both meaningful academic instruction and high- quality vocational training. Many of our vocational 
programs also require postsecondary education and industry-recognized credentials, making strong academic 
preparation essential for student success. We are proud of our students’ accomplishments in the classroom as 
well as in their vocational shops. 
Monty Tech students continue to demonstrate strong academic and postsecondary readiness outcomes while 
participating in a rigorous vocational -technical education. In the spring of 2025, 10th- grade students 
participated in the MCAS assessments in English Language Arts, Mathematics, and Biology. In addition, 
select 9th -grade students also took the MCAS Biology exam. Overall, students performed well across all 
subject areas, achieving passing rates of 97% in English Language Arts, 95% in Mathematics, and 95% in 
Biology. From an accountability perspective, Monty Tech continues to make moderate progress toward state 
targets, with average scaled scores exceeding 500 in all three subjects. Students performing at or above this 
level met grade- level expectations and are academically on track for success in their current 
coursework.  These outcomes align with Chapter 74 and DESE expectations that vocational -technical 
students demonstrate academic proficiency comparable to their peers in comprehensive districts while 
preparing for postsecondary education, training, and career 
pathways. 
 
When compar
ing Monty Tech’s results with Massachusetts and global performance, student outcomes

Monty Tech students also demonstrated 
outstanding achievement on Advanced 
Placement (AP) exams. Participation 
continues to increase, with 192 students 
taking AP courses and a record total of 
292 AP exams administered.  
As a 
result, a record number of students—135 
in total—earned qualifying scores of 3 
or higher, making them eligible to 
receive college credit. These results 
reflect our continued commitment to 
providing rigorous academic 
opportunities that support both college 
and career pathways and prepare 
students for long-term success.

exceeded state and/or global qualifying score percentages in three subject areas: 
 
• AP Research: 100% of students earned qualifying scores 
• AP English Language & Composition: 86% of students earned qualifying scores 
• AP Environmental Science: 85.7% of students earned qualifying scores  
Notably, Monty Tech had 35 AP Scholars, nearly double the 19 AP Scholars 
recognized following the 2024 AP exams. AP Scholars earn qualifying scores of 3 or 
higher on at least three AP exams.  
Monty Tech continues to offer a broad range of Advanced Placement opportunities 
across multiple content areas, to include:  English: AP English Literature & 
Composition, AP English Language & Composition, English 10 AP Seminar 
(planned for 2025–2026); Mathematics: AP Calculus, AP Statistics, AP Pre-Calculus 
(planned for 2026 -2027); Science: AP Environmental Science, AP Chemistry, AP 
Physics; and Electives: AP Seminar, AP Research, AP Psychology. 
These results underscore the dedication of our staff and the effectiveness of district -wide curriculum 
alignment, instructional support, and targeted professional development. In addition, several Advanced 
Placement courses were taught by new instructors or teachers in their first few years of delivering AP 
content. As these instructors and newly implemented courses continue to develop, Monty Tech anticipates 
sustained improvement in student performance and exam outcomes over time.   
While AP exam scores play an important role in earning college credit, the greatest value of Advanced 
Placement courses lies in the academic experience of the curriculum itself. AP coursework engages students 
in college-level reading, writing, problem -solving, and critical thinking, helping them develop the skills and 
habits needed for success in postsecondary education. Through sustained inquiry and rigorous instruction, 
students gain confidence, resilience, and a deeper understanding of their subject areas —benefits that extend 
well beyond the exam. 
These efforts reflect the district’s commitment to equity and access by intentionally expanding high-quality 
Advanced Placement opportunities for a broader and more diverse group of students, supported through 
structured teacher mentoring, collaborative planning, and ongoing professional development aligned to AP 
frameworks and Massachusetts standards. During the 2024- 2025 school year, Monty Tech implemented a 
new AP Psychology course and plans to launch English 10 AP Seminar in the 2025–2026 school year. In 
addition, the continued incorporation of new and updated instructional materials and curricula to expand 
STEM opportunities —through PLTW, AP, and dual enrollment options, particularly in mathematics and 
science—remains a strong priority as the district strengthens inclusive college and career readiness pathways 
for all students. 
Monty Tech also continues to offer a variety of after -school dual enrollment opportunities, expanding access 
to school-day dual enrollment, with a particular focus on strengthening STEM pathways for students. 
Monty Tech maintains a long-standing partnership with Mount Wachusett Community College 
through the Introduction to Biotechnology course, providing students with the opportunity to 
earn college credit upon successful completion. 
114
===PAGE 119===
Monty Tech will further expand its post -secondary partnerships by offering college credit 
through a four-year institution for the first time. Students who successfully complete Concepts 
in Biology and Anatomy & Physiology will earn college credit through a partnership with 
Worcester State University. This milestone reflects the continued strengthening of Monty 
Tech’s postsecondary collaborations and underscores the district’s commitment to expanding 
equitable access to rigorous, college-level learning opportunities. 
While Monty Tech’s academic programs continue to evolve, the district maintains a strong commitment to 
equity and access by intentionally supporting and honoring the language proficiency of our multilingual 
students. Monty Tech is a proud participant in the Massachusetts State Seal of 
Biliteracy program, which recognizes multilingualism as an asset and promotes 
equitable access to postsecondary and career opportunities for English Learners. 
In its inaugural year of participation, Monty Tech demonstrated strong outcomes. 
All participating seniors earned the Massachusetts Seal of Biliteracy, with students 
earning the Seal in Spanish and French, and one student also earning the Seal of 
Distinction in Haitian Creole. Overall, 77% of test takers earned the Seal, reflecting 
a high level of language proficiency among participating students. In addition, a 
Monty Tech staff member earned the Seal of Biliteracy with Distinction in Spanish, further modeling the 
value of multilingualism within our school community.  
This DESE- recognized credential affirms students’ attainment of high levels of English proficiency 
alongside another language, strengthens college and career readiness, and enhances students’ 
competitiveness in the workforce and global economy. Monty Tech remains committed to providing English 
Learners with inclusive, standards -aligned instruction and multiple pathways to demonstrate mastery, while 
celebrating the linguistic and cultural diversity that enriches our school community. 
Finally, 2024- 2025 brought much- needed enhancements and renovations to Monty Tech’s science 
department.  A storage expansion project added new cabinetry to six science labs, with cabinets designed and 
constructed by Monty Tech students.  Seven of the school’s twelve science laboratories have received new 
laboratory cabinetry, instructional workstations, and specialized equipment to ensure alignment with current 
safety standards, instructional expectations, and the Massachusetts Curriculum Frameworks.  Each year, 
science instructors work closely with the Director of Academic Programs to determine facility and 
professional development needs that would enhance the student experience and improve outcomes.  With 
continued support from Massachusetts Life Sciences Center, Monty Tech aims to further integrate state -of-
the-art technology into the science program, bringing students the latest technology.

Introducing students to new technology and learning platform s that include 3D gross and micro -anatomy

There is something special about a Massachusetts vocational school.  Our students have chosen this path to 
not only grow and learn in a traditional academic sense, but also to develop lifelong skills in one of the 
twenty-one vocational programs available.  Our vocational instructors are expert tradesmen and women, 
first.  They are proud of their vocations and have a vested interest in training the next generation of their 
workforce.  Each year, students are introduced to a variety of hands -on learning opportunities, that may 
occur on campus (fixing and replacing lights or the water fountains in the hallways) or off -campus 
(completely retrofitting an old warehouse to create a state -of-the-art training space).  Their high school years 
are marked with successes that may point back to years from now, knowing they have made an impact here 
at Monty Tech and in their own communities. The following are some of the more notable accomplishments 
across our vocational programs. 
 
Advanced Manufacturing:  Advanced Manufacturing students now work and learn in a state -of-the-art 
machine shop and have the opportunity to earn a number of industry- recognized credentials to demonstrate 
their technical capacity. All junior students completed the NC3 PMI 6 Certification.   These students have six 
stackable credentials in measuring. The seniors have earned six stackable credentials in Advanced Measuring 
Instrument this year, which expands upon the PMI credentials into more advanced measuring skills. Over 
two hundred credentials in metrology were earned. All Freshman students successfully completed safety 
training earning Career Safe OSHA 10 -hour general industry certification. We completed many projects for 
the community including tags for the Royalston Fire Department, and a plaque for the Town of Ashburnham. 
Fourteen Seniors and eleven Juniors earned co -op placements, working and learning in area machine shops. 
(Total student enrollment: 50) 
  
Auto Body Collision Repair Technology:   Monty Tech Auto Body students earned valuable industry 
credentials in the 2024-2025 School year. Students earned the OSHA 10-hour general industry card, EPA 6H 
spray certification, and ALI Certification. Students also earned I -CAR Certifications in Collision Repair 
Fundamentals, Disassembly and Re-assembly, Plastic Repair, Aluminum and Steel Small Dent Removal, and 
Preparation for Refinishing. Seven Seniors and three juniors earned co -op placements and were reported to 
be valuable contributors to local repair shops in the area. One Junior was placed on externship at the Gardner 
Fire Dept. At the SkillsUSA District Competition, seven out of nine students won a medal, earning a silver 
and gold in Auto Refinishing and Collision Repair. Two students were able to attend the SkillsUSA National 
Competition. We maintained several school vans, and painted multiple railings, stairs, and brackets for our 
MVP building. We painted a UTV for the Gardner Fire Department, repaired water leaks from a Petersham 
Police car, and repaired a car for Second Chance Cars to be given to a local family in need. We are currently 
working on a 1968 Ford Bronco, which will be auctioned off, and all proceeds will be donated to the Teen 
Challenge of New England.  (Total student enrollment: 67) 
  
Automotive Technology:  In Automotive Technology we 
serviced, repaired, and diagnosed approximately three hundred 
vehicles throughout the year.  These jobs included basic 
maintenance, inspections, alignments, brakes, exhaust, tires, and 
more. We also serviced the Petersham Police Department 
vehicle. The Upperclassmen earned their NC3/Snap on Apollo 
D8 Certification, Juniors earned their NC3/Snap on Multimeter 
certification, and the freshmen students earned the Career Safe

===PAGE 121===
OSHA 10 Certification. Seven Seniors and one junior earned a Co- op placement. For the SkillsUSA 
Competition, our Junior students placed third and fifteenth. While two Seniors placed fifth and fifteenth in 
the State Competition.   (Total student enrollment: 69)  
  
Business Technology: Monty Tech’s Business Technology program continues to thrive through its financial 
literacy initiatives. All Juniors and Seniors earned a financial literacy certificate through the MassMutual 
Foundation FutureSmart program. This year, one student represented the program in the Customer Service 
competition at the SkillsUSA District level. In addition, four Seniors and seven Juniors secured co -op 
placements for the 2024–2025 school year. Freshmen and Sophomores successfully completed the Career 
Safe OSHA 10 -hour training and CareerSafe Interview Skills modules. The department also engaged in 
several collaborative projects: a Macy’s Day Parade project with Information Technology, an Oreo Cookie 
Redesign project with Culinary and Advanced Manufacturing, and an International Hotel Project with CAD 
Drafting & Design. Weekly case studies have been introduced to highlight companies, product development, 
and international relations. These activities foster teamwork, communication, and collaboration both within 
Business Technology and across other technical areas. (Total student enrollment: 58) 
  
Cabinetmaking: Monty Tech Cabinetmaking students are called upon for a number of projects small and 
large, across the campus and in our communities. During the 2024- 2025 school year, students completed 
projects that included: build and install cabinets and countertops for the MVP Academy, build and install 
cabinets and countertops for the new IT office, and the existing dental shop. A number of projects were made 
for charitable gifts such as, flag shadow boxes for veterans, a toy box for the Christmas toy drive, and a coat 
storage unit for the Monty Tech Foundation. Fourteen Seniors & Thirteen Juniors earned co- op placements, 
representing the program well in the local workforce. Cabinetmaking freshmen completed OSHA Careersafe 
10 hr online course. (Total student enrollment: 70) 
 
CAD/Drafting & Design:  In 2024-2025, five Monty Tech CAD/Drafting & Design Senior students and one 
Junior earned Co-op Placement. Students were also able to achieve a number of valuable industry-recognized 
certifications including Autodesk Inventor, Revit, Autodesk AutoCAD and SolidWorks. Freshmen students 
completed the 10hr OSHA CareerSafe Online course. All students earned the Fundamental of Building 
Green Certifications. Two students competed in the SkillsUSA District Competition. The shop completed a 
variety of signs, banners, and posters for the school. We collaborated with Business Technology for a hotel 
design project and website. We designed the addition to the Phillipston Animal Control Facility. (Total 
student enrollment: 45) 
  
Cosmetology:  The Monty Tech Cosmetology program is one of the only programs that students may leave 
and directly enter the industry, fully-licensed and ready for a career.  The state-approved program is rigorous 
and provides students with countless opportunities to refine their cosmetology skills on clientele who 
understand and appreciate the learning environment. Freshmen completed the Career Safe Online OSHA 
course. Eighteen senior students earned their cosmetology licenses. Seven students earned Co- op Placement 
opportunities. One Senior placed Gold in SkillsUSA Districts and went to the SkillsUSA State Competition. 
Students earned certification in Pravana Color Certification, barbicide, and dermalogica certifications. Two 
Junior students went to SkillsUSA State Competition. Freshmen and Sophomores participated in Family & 
Friends Service Day. We participated in Beauty Battles at Worcester Voke, in which several sophomores 
won 1st & 2nd in nails and 1st in textured hair. One junior 
placed 1st for formal hair. (Total student enrollment: 85) 
  
Culinary Arts:  Monty Tech Culinary Arts students and 
instructors operate a full-service restaurant and bakery, serving 
70-90 patrons daily.  In September, they hosted a Welcome 
Back/New Teachers reception serving hors d’oeuvres and mini 
pastries for 60 people after school.  They prepared a luncheon 
for Principals & Counselors Day for 30 people in

October.  They prepared dinner for both Program Advisory Dinner/meetings for over 380 people each time.

of Fitchburg. Freshmen earned their Career Safe OSHA 10 hour Culinary certification, sophomores & 
juniors earned their Allergen Awareness certification, and juniors and seniors earned their Servsafe 
certification. One Senior competed at the state level in the SkillsUSA cooking competition. Junior students 
toured Johnson & Wales University of Providence RI. Sophomores students went to Great Wolf Lodge in 
Fitchburg where they toured the property and learned about the hotel industry and career opportunities.  
(Total student enrollment: 66)  
  
Dental Assisting: Monty Tech Dental Assisting instructors have emphasized the need for students to earn 
valuable industry-recognized credentials, as a means to demonstrate technical skill and proficiency.  In 2024-
2025, students obtained their certificate from CDC for Infection Control. Sophomores & Seniors received 
certification from American Red Cross for Basic Life support, and all freshmen received the Career Safe 10 
Hour OSHA. Eight Seniors and seven Juniors earned co- op placements, while the remaining Juniors and 
Seniors earned experience in affiliation/externship in area dental clinics and offices. Through the Community 
Health Connections Caring for Kids Program, Monty Tech students could receive free dental cleaning, 
radiographs, and sealants.  Our students were allowed to assist in their procedure. Junior students provided 
dental education for the pre-school children at the Guild of St. Agnes at Devens and Head Start in Fitchburg. 
One Senior received a gold medal to go to SkillsUSA states competition. (Total student enrollment: 60) 
  
Early Childhood Education:  Seven Seniors & eight Juniors earned co- op placements, gaining valuable 
experience working with young children in local childcare centers. Two Seniors and one Junior gained 
experience on externships. All Sophomore & Seniors completed their Pediatric and Adult First 
Aid/CPR/AED training.  Four Seniors have earned the Child Development Associate National certification. 
Six Seniors earned the MA Department of Early Education and Care Teacher certification. Nine juniors 
completed the 12 hour MA Strongstart Early Education and Care Essential Training. We hosted a mock baby 
shower with donations of diapers & wipes being donated to Montachusett Opportunity Council to benefit 
families in the community. Sophomores & juniors assisted in Head Start Preschool classrooms to acquire 
training hours for the national certification. Three Juniors went to SkillsUSA Districts, one junior placed 
bronze at States. All Freshmen completed the Career Safe 10 hr OSHA. We went on a field trip to the Eric 
Carle Museum to learn about children's literature and illustration and another Field trip to Turn BackTime (a 
nature-based education center) in Paxton to explore outdoor classroom environments. (Total student

The Monty Tech Cooperative Education Program is an extension of the student’s technical education that 
combines classroom instruction  with on-the-job-training.  The popular Co-op Program provides students 
with an opportunity to further develop academic, technical and employability skills in an industry- related

===PAGE 126===
work environment. All students are eligible to participate in  the Co-op Program, provided they satisfy state 
and school grade, attendance and performance requirements, and all Co-op students must have completed the 
OSHA recognized Career Safe online health and safety course prior to being placed in a work environment. 
 The Cooperative Education Program is beneficial for both students  and employers.  For the student, it is a 
chance to gain on-the-job training  that improves both their technical proficiency  and employability skills, 
and making industry connections enhances post -graduate employment opportunities.  The Co -op Program 
also allows students to earn while they learn, which has allowed many students to learn valuable life skills, 
financial literacy skills, and gain a deeper understanding of the importance of career advancement and self -
advocacy. 
  
Employer benefits include addressing workforce needs in an efficient manner.  Co -op students provide a 
pool of trained talent, ready to assume temporary, part -time or even full -time opportunities, thus reducing 
employer training costs.   Co-op work hours may also be tailored to suit the needs of partnering employers. 
  
With approximately 100 area employers participating in Cooperative Education, providing valuable 
experience to 286 students by the end of the 2024/2025 school year, school officials remain grateful for their 
continued support, and look forward to future collaborations.  
    
 
 
Monty Tech’s Department of Student Support Services houses the district’s school counseling services, 
oversees special education programming, and maintains efforts to provide social/emotional learning and 
resources to all students.  Throughout the 2025-2026 school year, department staff continued to prioritize the 
district's effort to educate and develop the next generation 
of young adults, ensuring students have access to support in 
academic and vocational programming, as well as services 
related to social and emotional wellness.   
 
Throughout an individual's high school career, Monty Tech 
offers numerous opportunities and support for individuals 
to explore vocational interests, develop skills, and make 
strides in personal growth and independence.  All students 
establish goals and identify pathway interests that align 
with DESE’s MyCAP (My Career and Academic Plan) 
initiative. Additionally, our trained and compassionate staff 
work to provide structured support for students as they 
acclimate to the demands, pace and rigor of high school 
academics, vocational programming and extracurricular activities.   
 
Balancing curriculum and course pathways with current social norms, Monty Tech recognizes young adults 
are growing up in a world that is rapidly changing.  With increased exposure to technology and social media, 
the means in which young adults communicate and connect has shifted.  With that, the foundational skills 
and social norms have also changed, taking on an array of variances that schools must recognize and address 
to effectively prepare students to be successful young adults.  
  
Recognizing the shift in experiences and brain development, Monty Tech has focused support on the 
growing concerns and struggles with age -appropriate interactions, use of social media, and the need for 
meaningful connections. The Department of Student Support has concentrated its efforts on creating a safe 
and supportive environment, offering opportunities and experiences that challenge our students to reflect, 
122
===PAGE 127===
grow, and learn in all areas of their experiences.  The department continued its important mission to break 
the stigma of mental health, shifting our perspective to find balance and mental wellness. This unified 
approach, support, and collaboration helps our student body develop socially, build resilience, communicate 
effectively, grow individually and meet success.      
  
 
Monty Tech has joined forces with CareSolace , matching individuals with mental 
health and/or substance abuse counselors.  CareSolace Coordinators address the 
growing need to locate support and match individuals with therapists, saving students 
and families hours of time, phone calls, waitlists and frustration.  CareSolace 
supports the matching process and follows up with individuals to ensure each 
connection was a success.   Most importantly, CareSolace Match Services are free to 
our students, family, staff and community: https://caresolace.com/site/montytech.    
  
During the 2024 -2025 school year, Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School District provided 
specialized services to approximately three hundred students – measuring progress of over two hundred 
students on Individual Education Plans (IEPs) and approximately one hundred students adhering to 
individualized Section 504 plans.  While the Student Support Services Department encompasses special 
education, the department provides support that is available to all Monty Tech students. Students have access 
to direct tutoring both before and after school.   School counselors are available to assist students with 
vocational placement and post-secondary planning.  Furthermore, our counselors are equipped with the skills 
to engage students in workshops and groups that support the development of social resilience, functional 
academics, finding balance, and referral services for those experiencing struggles.   
 
  
  
 
 
Keeping up with technology is essential to any industry, but in vocational education, it is critical.  Students 
and staff access traditional platforms, found and used often in school districts across the nation, but are also 
introduced to industry-specific technology and tools that demand mastery prior to entering the workforce. 
 
The Monty Tech Technology Department is a crucial 
department that not only keeps technology operating, but 
they consistently seek and secure the latest platforms, 
operations, and applications to streamline education, 
training, and day- to-day work efforts.  In 2024 -2025, the 
district’s Technology Director attend ed the LDR512: Cybersecurity Leadership Conference.  There he 
gained advanced technical and leadership skills to effectively manage the district’s security programs and 
learned more about key topics like security architecture, vulnerability management, cloud security, and 
GenAI security.   
 
The Commonwealth of Massachusetts provided a state match through a well -deserved Municipal Local 
Cybersecurity Grant, making this opportunity possible.  As a result, the Monty Tech Technology Department 
is better equipped and more prepared to successfully manage evolving threats, quickly address i ncident 
responses, identify hacker tools and techniques, and understand and share cyber threat intelligence to the 
school community.   
 
Supporting and maintaining student devices and instructional technology continues to comprise the bulk of 
123
===PAGE 128===
the department’s efforts.  With each student having a school -issued Chromebook, and academic and 
vocational teachers using a wide variety of instructional technology, the talented technology team continues 
to streamline troubleshooting and support efforts to ensure more than 1,600 student and staff are online daily, 
and able to fully participate in educational activities at all times.

The Monty Tech Marine Corps Junior Reserve Officer Training Corps (MCJROTC) is a leadership program 
focused on instilling values of citizenship, service, personal responsibility, and accomplishment in young 
adults. Throughout the 2024- 2025 school year, cadets served over 3,000 hours in community service and 
volunteerism, highlighting their growth and increased efforts in community outreach. 
 
  
 
During the 2024 - 2025 school year, twenty- six cadets attended a joint Cadet Leadership Course (CLC) in 
southern Massachusetts, where they engaged in team -building activities and challenged their mental and 
physical capabilities through obstacle courses and squad competitions. This four -day camp fostered 
camaraderie and leadership development with cadets from other JROTC programs in New England. 
 
Additionally, cadets supported three community 5K runs, including the Friends of Fitchburg Veterans 5K, 
the Rock and Roll 5K at Wachusett Brewery, and the Heroes Live Forever 5K. They provided assistance 
with parking, run route coordination, and vendor support. Participation in the annual Blacksmith Festival in 
Fitchburg, MA, offered them insights into community volunteerism and the craft of blacksmithing. 
 
In collaboration with the Fitchburg Salvation Army, cadets participated as bell ringers and conducted 
volunteer tolls, successfully collecting over $25,000 in donations to support the less fortunate in the Monty 
Tech community. This fundraising goes on to assist with the costs associated with clothing, housing, food, 
and transportation requirements for numerous community members. 
 
The Monty Tech Color Guard supported in excess of 25 color guards at numerous events, including Monty 
124
===PAGE 129===
Tech home football games, community, and veteran ceremonies, as well as graduate and undergraduate 
celebrations at Fitchburg State University. 
 
A special Veterans Day ceremony on November 10th honored Monty Tech staff and student veteran family 
members through a collage display and sword detail presentation. Also, on November 10th, the MCJROTC 
celebrated the 250th birthday of the United States Marine Corps with a Birthday Ball. Over 250 cadets and 
guests attended for a four-course meal, music, and dancing.  
 
Cadets have also participated in several different competitions including National Marksmanship 
competitions, cyber- security competitions, and numerous Raider challenges with an emphasis on confidence 
and discipline. 
 
This year emphasized the cadets' well -rounded approach to continued improvements and dedication to 
community outreach and volunteerism, as well as team building and small unit leadership development. 
 
 
 
In recent years,  the Mont y Tech School of Continuing Education, has received 
more than $2.7M in funding from Commonwealth Corporation, to provide 
valuable workforce training at no-cost for individuals who may be unemployed or 
underemployed.  These grant-funded training opportunities are an example of the 
collaboration between the MassHire Workforce Investment Board, MassHire 
North Central Career Center, the Executive Office of Labor and Workforce 
Development, and generous sponsorship from Commonwealth Corporation, 
making it possible for Monty Tech to offer meaningful job readiness training 
in occupations with high demand and self-sustaining wages. 
  
In 2024- 2025, school leaders were pleased to offer 200- hour training programs in Automotive Collision 
Repair, Carpentry, Plumbing, and HVAC to qualified participants.  Of the 64 students who enrolled, 56 
participants persisted in completing their selected training program.  Of those, 21 having since secured full -
time employment in their chosen occupations.  In addition to these grant-funded opportunities, the School of 
Continuing Education, continues to offer the area’s most affordable and comprehensive cosmetology training 
program, effectively preparing participants to sit for the Massachusetts State Board examinations.  In FY25, 
nine cosmetology students graduated from the program.  
  
Serving more than 1,700 community members annually, the School of Continuing Education is proud to 
offer training to a non -traditional student population, while working to develop new and important 
relationships with business partners in need of a talented candidate pool.  The Continuing Education staff 
understands that the strength of any vocational training program is dependent upon mutually beneficial 
relationships between the school and our business partners, and they work tirelessly to build relationships 
throughout the year.  In May 2025, the School of Continuing Education hosted the annual Job Fair, 
welcoming more than thirty area employers who spent time visiting with both day and evening students 
seeking job opportunities.  Numerous students attended the event, presented resumes to business 
representatives, and made contacts with employers that led to a first -time job placement and entry into the 
trades. Additionally, the School of Continuing Education hosted its first annual Fall and Spring Open 
Houses, designed to offer prospective students the opportunity to learn more about training programs, meet 
instructors and ask questions.   
 
125
===PAGE 130===
The Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School Practical Nursing Program class of 2025 achieved 
a remarkable 93% NCLEX-PN Pass Rate on their first attempt, exceeding the national first- time pass rate of 
86.6% for U.S.- educated Practical Nursing candidates.  This achievement highlights the hard work and 
commitment of our students, along with the ongoing support and guidance from our faculty and staff. 
 
Our success starts with a learning environment that is supportive, student -focused, and built to help students 
grow into confident, capable nurses. Our faculty play a key role —not only teaching in the classroom and 
clinical setting but also mentoring students throughout the program. Coursework and clinical training are 
designed to strengthen clinical judgment and build a solid understanding of nursing practice. We emphasize 
evidence-based practice, critical thinking, patient safety, and compassionate, culturally aware care. 
 
We also know that student success includes more than academics. The program offers strong student support 
services, including academic advising, wellness resources, and assistance navigating financial assistance. 
Faculty advisors work closely with students, providing consistent encouragement and mentorship from day 
one through graduation. 
 
To continue expanding clinical learning opportunities, we are actively building new community clinical 
partnerships to give students experience in a variety of healthcare environments. These efforts support our 
goal of growing the program and preparing Practical Nurses who are ready to meet workforce needs across 
North Central Massachusetts. 
 
Ongoing improvement is a priority for our program. We regularly review and update our curriculum, invest 
in faculty development, and expand student resources to support learning and licensure success. We will 
continue incorporating technology and evidence-based teaching strategies to help students graduate prepared 
and career-ready. 
 
Community interest in the program remains strong, with more than 150 inquiries each year for 48 available 
seats in our annual cohort. 
 
Our graduates have successfully transitioned into nursing roles in many local healthcare settings within our 
school district, to include the following employer partners: 
 
• CAPS Collaborative • MA Department of Developmental Services  
• The Highlands • Fitchburg Healthcare 
• Gardner Rehabilitation and 
Nursing 
• Wachusett Manor 
• Heywood Hospital • Spring Hill Recovery 
• Athol Hospital • MCI-Gardner

What may be considered the single greatest 
accomplishment of the 2024- 2025 school year 
was not achieved on our campus, but rather one 
mile down the road.  After securing $3.4M in 
competitive grant funding, school leaders 
celebrated the opening of the MVP Academy, the 
district’s first satellite campus, designed in 
partnership with Fitchburg, Gardner, and 
Narragansett Regional Public Schools, to provide 
vocational training in Carpentry, Electrical and 
Plumbing (beginning F all 2026)  to interested 
students.  Renovating an empty 16,000 sq. ft. 
warehouse to create an all -new training academy, 
provided meaningful training and skill -building 
opportunities for our own students, who 
completed a majority of the impressive work.   
 
A combined 72 students (Juniors and Seniors) from our partnering districts participated in the Academy’s 
inaugural year.   Led by expert carpenters and electricians, MVP students work every day to prepare for 
careers in a variety of occupations.  Students use equipment and technology funded entirely by grants, 
earning valuable industry-recognized credentials before graduating. 
 
Juniors and Seniors in Electrical began by completing ladder safety training and shop safety exercises, before 
advancing to complete week -long hands-on projects in their own booths, demonstrating safe practices and 
127
===PAGE 132===
skill development.  Following industry- standard Mike Holt curriculum, Seniors also satisfied Year 1 of the 
Electrical Apprentice Classroom Instruction hours, streamlining entry into this high- demand, high- wage 
occupation.  Juniors and Seniors in the Carpentry trade learned about foundations and footings, while 
adhering to the Massachusetts Carpenters Unions curriculum.  They, too, completed shop safety training 
prior to using any power tools, and became skilled in cutting, shaping, and construction foundation skills.   
Employers visited with instructors and students to guide learning and encourage job placements upon 
graduation, which motivated students and only reaffirmed the relevance 
of MVP training. 
 
In April 2025, MVP Academy welcomed state and local leaders, 
vocational educators from across the Commonwealth, students and even 
proud parents to the Ribbon Cutting Ceremony. The celebration marked 
not only completion of a building, but also new and life -changing 
opportunities now available to regional students.  Student speakers 
spoke of a high school experience that lacked engagement before 
attending MVP, where they found renewed spirit and meaning in all 
lessons, including those academic lessons they were previously 
disinterested in.  The celebration further substantiated school leaders’ 
commitment to expanding access and marked a significant step in 
addressing the chronic lack of seats in vocational training programs in 
our region.  Since that celebration, school leaders have been asked to 
speak at national conferences, provide tours of the facility for those who 
did not attend, and even offer guidance in grant applications that may 
fund a similar satellite campus and partnership program, in districts 
across Massachusetts.   
The district continues to benefit from a talented leadership team whose varied educational 
backgrounds, professional experiences, and areas of expertise contribute to the success of the school. 
Working collaboratively, and under the direction of the Superintendent , the team has transformed the 
school into one of the most sought-after high schools in North Central Massachusetts.

TOWN MEETING EXCERPTS: 
Annual Town Meeting - May 3, 2025 
 
The Moderator called the meeting to order at 9:03 AM declaring a quorum, announced that the warrant was 
properly served, then led the assembly in the Pledge of Allegiance. He then went over ground rules of the 
meeting, then made announcement about vacancies on various committees. Flowers were presented to Tom 
Alonzo and Michael Ray Jefferies for their years of service to the town as selectmen. Various Town and School 
employees were recognized for their years of service. The new police chief, Jeffrey Thibodeau was welcomed. 
The Moderator then had the meeting test the electronic devises for recording the vote taken on each article. 
Robert Bowen was made the Deputy Moderator and the Moderator explained the Consent Calendar and moved 
for consideration Articles 4, 5, 18, 21, 23, 24, 25, 26, 27, 32, and 33. The Consent Calendar was passed 
unanimously by voice vote. Finance Committee and Select Board recommend Approval. 
 
ARTICLE 1:  To see if the Town will vote to hear and/or accept the regular written reports of the Town 
Officers and Committees; or take any other action relative thereto. Heard Select Board Report on Brooks 
House and Storm Water Task Force by DPW Director. 
 
ARTICLE 2:  To see if the Town will vote to authorize the Town Treasurer/Tax Collector, with the approval of 
the Select Board, to borrow money from time to time in anticipation of the revenue of the Fiscal Year 2026, the 
period from July 1, 2025 through June 30, 2026, in conformity with the provisions of the Massachusetts General 
Laws, Chapter 44, Section 4, and to issue a note or notes as may be given for a period of less than one year in 
accordance with Massachusetts General Laws,  Chapter 44, Section 17, and to authorize the Treasurer/Tax 
Collector to enter into a compensating balance agreement or agreements for Fiscal Year 2026 pursuant to 
Massachusetts General Laws, Chapter 44, Section 53F; or take any other action relative thereto.  VOTED (yes 
145; no 25; abstain 1, 114 needed for 2/3rds majority) to authorize the Town Treasurer/Tax Collector, 
with the approval of the Select Board, to borrow money from time to time in anticipation of the revenue 
of the Fiscal Year 2026, the period from July 1, 2025 through June 30, 2026, in conformity with the 
provisions of the Massachusetts General Laws,  Chapter 44, Section 4, and to issue a note or notes as may 
be given for a period of less than one year in accordance with Massachusetts General Laws,  Chapter 44, 
Section 17, and to authorize the Treasurer/Tax Collector to enter into a compensating balance agreement 
or agreements for Fiscal Year 2026 pursuant to Massachusetts General Laws,  Chapter 44, Section 53F.  
Finance Committee and Select Board recommend Approval.

ARTICLE 15  To see if the Town will vote to amend the Code of Lunenburg, Chapter 70, entitled “Salary 
Administration Plan,” Section 70-19, entitled “Paid Holidays”, by amending Section C as follows (additions are 
shown in bold italics for emphasis only); or take any other action relative thereto: 
 
C. Holiday leave will be granted on the day the Town observes the holiday, when possible. If the holiday falls 
on a Saturday, it will be observed on the preceding Friday. If the holiday falls on a Sunday, it will be observed 
on the following Monday. If an eligible employee is required to work on the day the Town observes the holiday, 
they will be entitled to a Floating Day, which has to be used within 30 days, by agreement of the supervisor and 
employee. If the department head determines that holiday leave cannot be granted, the employee shall receive 
payment at one and one-half times the base hourly rate for all hours worked on the holiday, in addition to his or 
her holiday pay. Final determination is to be made by the Town Manager.  Call Firefighters assigned to work a 
Duty Shift at the Fire Station on a holiday shall be paid one and one half times their hourly rate for the 
hours worked. For purposes of this section only, Holiday time shall be considered the calendar date of the 
holiday, regardless of the day of the week it falls on.  VOTED (Yes 123, No 7) to amend the Code of 
Lunenburg, Chapter 70, entitled “Salary Administration Plan,” Section 70-19, entitled “Paid Holidays”, 
by amending Section C as follows: 
 
C. Holiday leave will be granted on the day the Town observes the holiday, when possible. If the holiday 
falls on a Saturday, it will be observed on the preceding Friday. If the holiday falls on a Sunday, it will be 
observed on the following Monday. If an eligible employee is required to work on the day the Town 
observes the holiday, they will be entitled to a Floating Day, which has to be used within 30 days, by 
agreement of the supervisor and employee. If the department head determines that holiday leave cannot 
be granted, the employee shall receive payment at one and one -half times the base hourly rate for all 
hours worked on the holiday, in addition to his or her holiday pay. Final determination is to be made by 
the Town Manager.  Call Firefighters assigned to work a Duty Shift at the Fire Station on a holiday shall 
be paid one and one half times their hourly rate for the hours worked. For purposes of this section only, 
Holiday time shall be considered the calendar date of the holiday, regardless of the day of the week it falls 
on. Finance Committee and Select Board recommend Approval.
